monthly report for January 1939.
The section of the report dealing with
relations of the United States contains no
information not already received in the Divi-
sion by despatch or telegram except possibly
under the heading "General", where the state-
ment is made that a stronger adverse feeling
toward the United States was discernible
during January and where a surmary is given
of an article in the Tokyo Nichi Nichi which
is "perhaps an accurate expose of the official
attitude in Japan toward the United States"
This summary is as follows:
"That article attributed to the Foreign
Office the view that America's taking steps
of economic reprisal against Japan is merely
a gesture to restrain Japanese activities in
China and that the United States together with
Great Britain lack determination to put such
measures into practice. It was held that the
Foreign Office authorities assuned that such
counter-activities of the United States and
Great Britain will not be effective; for, in
the first place, Japan's insistence to create
a new order in East Asia is a just one and
will be acceptable to the United States and
Britain if only it is properly explained.
Secondly, although Britain evidently desires
the United States to take positive measures
towards

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
towards bringing economic pressure on Japan
and although cooperation between the United
States and Great Britain in executing their
foreign polioies may grow closer through their
strong political, commercial and economio
bond, nevertheless in the United States there
stili persists a strong isolationist sentiment,
many people are opposed to an anti-Japanese
policy, and consequently Anglo-American unity
in adopting anti-Japanese economic measures
has not yet been established."
Reaction to the British note of January 14
(in regard to rights and interests in China)
was "one of hearty displeasure" (page 8).
The Frenoh note on the same subject was
also received with hostile comment (page l2).
The seotion dealing with military condi-
tions in China and with Sino-Japanese relations
(pages 14-16) contaim no material of signifi-
cance new to the Division.
The Japanese press reported that the
Japanese in Brazil cultivating jute are being
suocessful (page l8); that the Brazilian Gov-
ernment has invited io0 Japanese families to
settle in Brazil to cultivate jute; and that
the State of Para intends to lease 25,000 acres
for the growing of jute by a Japanese organiza-
tion and to invite 5o0 immigrants a year for
the growing of jute thereon.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Reaction to the United States' note of
December 30, 1938.
The American note of December 30, 1938, was featured
in the Japanese press on January 2, l939. The reaction to the
7/1.94
note was restrained and, overtly at least, not greatly concern-
ed over the opinion and position of the American Government.
The General attitude taken was one of passing over the American
stand as set forth in the note, maintaining that the United
States' nominal insistence on old formulas shows failure to
appreciate the present situation in the Far East, the peculiar
economic and political conditions in this part of the world,
and Japan's position in China. Comments were made that the
American policy is based on theoretical diplomacy - not on
actualities - and that Japan cannot accept this policy. It was
also stated that an international confereice on the current
situation in China would inevitably mean a concert by Great
Britain, France and the United States to check Japan and that
such action would be intolerable. However, expressions of regret
were proffered that the United States still remains dissatis-
fied with Japan's attitude and it was urged, therefore, that
Japan must continue to endeavor to convince America of the
justice of Japan's policy pending change of American opinion
for the better.*
An article appeared in one of the leading local papers,
anticipating a move by the Foreign Minister toward seeking
settlement of questions involving foreign rights and interests
in China by demonstrating that Japan does not intend to close
the Door in China, and at the same time making it clear to
the
* Embassy's despatch No. 3612, January 17, 1939.

.2.
the American and British Governmonts that exceptions in the
principle of equal opportunity will heve to be made in order
to permit of the development of the economic self-sufficiency
of China, Japan, nd "Manchukuo". But the Forcign Offico
stated thnt the articlo was not insnired,* and, although in
his Diet speech on Jonuary 2l the Foreign Minister referred in
general terms to dissent from abroed to the objcctives of the
Japonese policy as sct forth, no officicl statement directly
concerning the American note was made during the month.
(b) Alaskan Slmon Fisheries.
In conformity with the Department's instructions,
the Ambassador directed the Counsclor of Embassy to take up
informally with the Foreign Office the question of the Alaskan
salmon fisheries. On Janunry 1l Mr. Dooman called on Mr.
Yoshizowa, tho Director of the American Section of the Foreign
Office, and r ferring to the arrangement entered into by the
Americen and Japanese Governments in Merch, 1938, pointed out
that the failure of the Japnnese Government to give a binding
undert king to prevent its nationals from fishing for salmon off
the coast of Alaska had led to a growing sense of insecurity
on the pert of Americans interusted in salmon fishery ond he
hoped that the Japanese Government would be disposed to con-

Yoshizawe expressed great surprise that the matter should have
been brought up ag in and stated that he could not understand

(c) Canton Cust a s:
It is recalied tnat ca November 24, 1938, in cenformity
with the Department's instrucii r vhe Ambassador presented a
note to the Japanese Foreign Office pretesting the taking ovor
by the Japanese censular and milita:y autherities of the Chinese
Maritime Custons at Canton on November 9. Similar notes were
presented by the British and French Ambassadors.
The respective
raplies received from the Japanese Government to these notes
stated that "since the Canton area is at present occupied by
the Imperial Army it is proper that the Canton Maritime Customs
be placed under the authority of the Japanese Army of Occupation."
During the month under review the British Ambassader proposed
that the British, American and French Ambassadors make formal
parallel representations contesting the Japanese assertion of
the propriety of the Japanese military forces taking over the
Canton Customs. Inasmuch as the representations of this Embassy
to the Japanese Government with respect to interference with
the Chinese Customs Administration at various places in China
by Japanese authorities have consistently and implicitly pro-
tested action deriving from no cause other than that of action
nd operations by the Japenese military forces, it was felt
that the American Government's position had been made perfectly
clear
Embassy's despatch No. 3639, January 28, 1939.

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.98)
clear and that unless some unforeseen developments occurred
further formal representations would not be likely to bring
favorable practical results. However, in conformity with the
Department's telegraphic instructicns of January 4 authorizing
a separate and formal rojoinder to the Japanese assertion, the
Ambassador made to his colleagues a counter-proposal that,
instead of addressing written notes to the Foreign Office,
the three Ambassadors respectively orully observe that their
Governments cannot accept the Japanese contention that military
occupation of the Canton area offered justification for taking
over the Customs. This action was grsed upon and the observe-
tions are to be mnde by the Ambassadors in their next meetings
with the Foreign Minister.*
(a) General Relations.
No m jor developments in Americ n-Jupanese relations took
rlnce during the month under review and the outstanding issues
between the two countrius were, on the part of Japin, left in
abeyance. How ver, lthough the luadership in opposition to
Japan by the so-called democratic powers was still attributed
to Great Britain, a stron er adverse feoling toward the United
States wes discernible. Considerable attention was given in
the press to reports that the United Stites was considering
the imposing of cconomic sanctions against Jopan.
In the Diet
repeated roferenco was also mado to these reports nd in dis-
cussing this subject the members of the Diet cxpressed sentiments
of defience against any such action by foreign countries.
Perhaps on eccurate exposé of the officinl attitude in
japen towards the United States in regard to the subject of
conomic sanctions was made in an article published in the
TOKYO
Embassy's telegrams No. 3, Jonuary 2 nd No. 22, January 13,
nd Embassy's despatch No. 3646, January 30, 1939.

II. RELATICNS VITH OTHUR CCUNTRIES.
(a) Great Britain.
On January 14 the British Ambassador presented to the
Japanese Government a note, substantially along the lires of the
American note of December 30, setting forth the British position
with regard to British rights and interests in China as affected
741.94
by Japanese policy and actions. The British Ambassador, on
presenting
These representations were reported by the followine despetches
which are listed in the same order as the above Anerican repre-
sentations: Despatches No. 3619, Jan 18; 3621, Jan. 18; 3622.
Jan. 18; 3625, Jan. 18; 3629, Jan. 23; 3636, Jan. 26; 3632,

=== Page 15 === (confidence 0.98)
....
presenting the note to the Foreign Minister, informed the
latter that the note had been drafted in London before the
British Foreign Office had knowledge of the American note of
December 30.*
The complete text of the note was published in the press
and considerable editorial comment was given to it. Its gener-
al features may be summarized as follows: The British Govern-
ment informed the Japanese Government of the uncertainty and
grave anxiety in which it was left by a study of Japan's new
policy in the Far East. It outlined the intentions of the
Japanese Government in regard to Japan's policy towards China
and Manchuria as inferred from pronouncements made und official
informetion issued in Japan. It stated that the British Govern-
ment was at a loss to underst nd how the assur nce given by
the former Prime Minister, Prince Konoye, that Japan seeks no
territory and respects the sovereignty of Chine, are to be
reconciled with the declred intention of the Jepanese Govern-
ment to compel the Chinese people by force of arms to accept
conditions involving surrender of their political, economic,
nd cultural life to Japonese control, and with the indefinite
maintenance in China of consider ble Japanese garrisons and vir-
tual detachment from Chinn of the territory of Innur Mongolia.
The British Government is not prep red to uccept or recoenize
activities of the nature indic ted which are brouht bout by
force; it intends to dhere to the principle of the Nine Power
Trerty and c nnot gree to unilater-l modificntion of its
terms; and it c nnot agree, as suggested in Japan, that the Treaty
is obsolete or that its provisions no longer meet the situation,
except in so for as the situ tion has been altered by Japan in
contravention of its terms. But it adds that, while the British
Government.
Embassy's tulegram No. 16, January 9, 1939.

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.98)
-8-
Government maintains that modificntion cannot be effected uni-
laterally and must be by neeotiati on by ell the signetories,
it does not contend that treaties are eternal; and that, there-
fore, if the Japanese Government has any constructive suggestions
for the modification of the multilcteral agreements relating to
China, the British Government will be ready to consider them.
In the meantime, the British Government reserves all its rights
under existing treaties.*
The reaction to the British note was one of hearty
displeasure.** Reflective of this general feeling were state-
ments to the effect thot: If Brit in does not want to be
Japan's enemy by supporting Japan's foe, the Chiang Kai-shek
Government,she should cooperate with Japan in establishing a
new order in the Far East; Britain can come to an understand-
ing with Japan by fncing the fact that a new situntion hes
arisen in Enst Asin; and the British attitude s well as that
of America is overbearing, if not one of bluff, but such atti-
tude will not induce Japan to modify its policy.
Other comments, however, were noted which, although
following the same lines of nntipathy to the British stand,
suggested that either the British note, as also the American
note, should not be taken seriously or that the British con-
tention should be met by stressing the constructive phases
of Japan's undertaking and by making it clearer that Japanese
activities will not be conducted it the material cost of third
countries.

On January 14 the British Anbossedor aiso took up with
the Foreign Office the questicn of the reopening of the Yengtze
River to third party merchant vesspis. He informsd the Toveign
Minister that he hed heard thet sericus considelation was teing
given in Japanese official circles to the question of the ne-
opening of the river ond in order to strengthen the latter's
hands wished to leave with him a memorandum setting forth cer-
tain data that had been compiled by British sources regarding
Japanese trading on the Yangtze and safety of navig tion for
ships of other nation litics. The menorandum gave 43 reported
cases during the month of November wherein Japanese merchant
vessels traded at fanking, Wuhu and Kukiang and it contended
that the goods listed as being traded in were not in the main
merely carried as subsidiary cargoes on Japanese military trans-
ports thereby disproving the Japanese conuention made in Novem
ber that Japanese vessels trading on the Yangtze are without
exception engaged as military transports carrying primarily
war materials. In contradiction to the Japanese statement that
the Yangtze must remain closed owing to the presence of mines,
re oval of navigation marks, military operations, etc., it
was also stated therein that infermation received from the British
naval authorities on the Yangtce was that from the point of view
of safety there is no reason wny foreign steamers should not
trade between the sea and Wuhu and that, moreover, above Wuhu,
with the anticipated rise of the river in the course of the next
few weeks, the river should equally he accessible to foreign
vessels.*

This Embassy was informed about the middle of Januery
that the British Embassy had received from the Minister fcr
Foreign Affairs replies to the British representations of Decem-
ber 20 and 2o with regard to Japenese interference with the
British and French concessions at Tientsin. Their replies
stated that the Japanese action was considered necessary
because of anti-Japanese activities within the British con-
cession at Tientsin, and further that the matter was being
dealt with by the authorities at that city.*
Reports were continually published in the press that
Great Britain, along with the United States, is contemplating
the taking of economic reprisal egainst Japan because of her
policy on the Asiatic Continent, and that reprisal would most
likely be in the form of special taxes on Japanese imports
into the British Empire.
The opinion was generally held that
Great Britain would not act alone but only with the United States
in a united Anglo-Americon attack on Japan.. Feeling was bitterer
azrinst Great Britain then against the United States, as the in-
spiration and the primary desire for action of this kind were
attributed to the former country. Stronger and more antago-
nistic terms were used in proclsiming threats of counter-
reteliation for economic moves on the art
of Great Britain and
in asserting that any such moves would not affect Japanese policy.
It was reported in the press towards the end of the
month thot the Japanese Ambassador at London hed protested to
the British Government against mistrentment of Japanese in
Singopore. The alleged complaints appeared to have occurred
over a period of : year or more, and the press noting the
protest averred that the

Government intends to pursue in the Far East is not compatibie

The Government
of France points out that it coulå not accept or recognize any
modification of the existing order established in China by the
treaties in force which was not brought about by prior corsulta-
tion among the interested Powers anā duly accepted by them. It
continues to believe that the principles laid down in the Nine
Power Treaty have not lost their validit:. It refuses to believe
that the Japanese Government proposes unilaterally to denounce
the contractual undertakings to which it freely subscribed or
intends to confront the other Powers which are parties to these
agreements with a fait accompli of such a kind as gravely to
prejudice their interests. However, in case the Imperial Govern-
ment should believe that the course of events in the Far East
requires an adjustment of the aforesaid treaty the French
Government will be prepared to receive any suggestion made by
the Japanese Government.*
The note was published in full in the Japanese press.
A statement by the spokesman of the Foreign Office was reported
as characterizing the note as a gesture on the part of the
French Government toward following the lead taken by the United
States and British Governments.
The spokesman was reputed to
have said also that the Japanese Government does not place too
much inportance on the French note. Comment of a hostile nature
appeared in the press regarding this French representation, to
the effect that France is able to maintain its position as a
world power only throuh the support of Great Britain and America,
has no opinions of its own on world affairs especially as regards
the

(c) China.
i1.
Military Operations.*
The lull in Japanese military operations which was
noted in December appears to have continued during January,
during which month no major developments were reported.
Sporadic encounters with guerrilla bands occurred and some
operations undertaken on a limited scale against Chinese forces
on the fringes of the occupied areas.
The most imnortant military events occurred in southwest
793.94
Shansi, where two groups of Japanese units made drives north
and west from Hotsien and Linfen respectively in what con-
stituted presumably another "clean-up" campaign in that
area, and it was announced that through this campaign some
20,Oo0 Chinese were driven out of Shansi into Shensi. At
the same time the Japanese harassed the Chinese along the
Lunghai Railway in the form of artillery boabardment across
the Yellow River and also with intensive aerial bombing.
However, the long-anticipated drive of the Japanese across
the Yellow River and on to Sian did not materialize during
the month, which was possibly explained by the failure of the
Yellow River to freeze sufficiently to permit the passage
over it of heavy materiel.
In the Juhan area there were no engagements of any im-
portance, and although reports from some sources were to the
effect that the Japanese were quietly preparing for an offen-
sive on the Yochow-Nanchang front there appeared to have
been no moves initirted during the nonth to do else than
hold the territorial gains already made.

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a) The Cabinet.
The Cabinet of which Prince Fumimaro Konoye was Premier
resigned on January 4 and on January 5 a Cabinet under the
premiership of Baron Kiichiro Hiranuma was installed.
With the principal exceptions of Mr. Ikeda, who held con-
currently the office of Minister of Finance and Minister
of Commerce, and Admiral Suetsugu, who held the office
of Home Minister, the outstanding figures of the Konoye
854.c0
Cabinet were carried over into the new one. These were:
Marquis Kido, General Itagaki, Admiral Yonai, Mr. rita,
General Araki, and Mr. Shiono. Prince Konoye himself was
designated Minister without Portfolio in the Hiranuma Cabinet
while at the same time he became Baron Hiranuma's successor
as President of the Privy Council. The new Cabinet installed
on January 5 was made up as follows:
Prime Minister
Baron Kiichiro Hiranuma
Minister without Portfolio
Prince Fumimaro Konoye
Minister for Foreign Affairs

The Cabinet chane was effucted without specific public
knowledge of the causes that brought such change about and
Prince Konoye, as is customary, merely explained his resig-
natien in vague generalities. However, a careful study of
the political situntion prior to and following the change in-
dicates

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.98)
-C-
dicates that it may be attributed to the following rrincips!
causes:
A cleavage is believed to have arisen in the Cubines
because of insistence of Home Minister Suetsugu for measures
to be adpted for the "organizetion of the nation" through
strict regimentation. These measures were subjected to
debate in the Cabinet itself, they came to the attertion of
the leaders in the Diet who resented them, and trouble ahead
for the Government became evident.
Such a situation gave
the Prime Minister the opportunity to resign, which oppor-
tunity he had been desiring for some time. Added to this
there was a growing feeling in Japan that a change in leader-
ship was required in the Government to give a "lift" to popu-
lar enthusiasm in order to meet the task confronting the
nation. The country had gone tlhirough a long strain during
in the feeling nd ttitude of the public. With the conclusion
of what was termed nositi nnl military operations and the in-
auguration cf the so-called reconstruction st: ge at home and
on the continent it was felt that a fresh leader was required.*

V.
GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
The ordinary budget estimates for 1939-40 were submitted
to the Diet by the Finance Minister. The figure given for the
budget was ¥ 3,694,000,000, which represented an increase of
￥ 180,000,000 over that of 1938-39. 0rdinary revenues are to
be increased by Y294,000,000 as compared with the previous
year and are to be obtained primarily through increased taxes
of approximately ¥200,000,000; while a deficit of ¥1,082,000,000
is to be made up by bond issues.
Although it was stated that
a special supplementary budget to cover military expenditures
connected with the "China Incident" would be presented to the
current session of the Diet the amount of this budget was not
announced.

=== Page 33 === (confidence 0.96)
-25-
announced.
However, from other sources it was intimated thac
this latter budget might amount to some ¥6,000,000,000. It wi!i
be met presumably by bond issues. During 1938 Government hond
issues of ¥3,800,000,000 were f1ca:ed and taken up by ths public
and the private banks, while since she outbreak of the "Cine
Incident" some ¥5,600,000,000 bonds have been issued, By the
end of the current year if the present program is carried out
the increase in Japan's bond indebtedness attendant upcn the
hostilities in China will exceed ¥10,000,000,000.
The Finance Minister mentioned that some difficulty in
floating the issues was enccuntered lest fall but clcimed that
a better reception had been evidenced during January. In that
month bonds amounting to some ¥440,000,000 were issued and
absorbed.
VI.
GENERAL ECCNOMIC AND FINACIAL CONDITICNS.
Business sentiment during the month under review vas
uneasy due to the political situation. The prolongedhostilities
in Chirn, the ch nsc of Cabinet, the presentetion of the American,
British, and French notes on Jonan's China policy, the unsettled
Soviet fishery question and the disturbed political situation
in Europe grve rise to apprehen ion nd uncert inty. Reflective
of this situation, the  tock merket was unsettled and dull.
The Bank of Japan nete issue rerched a record seasonal peck
of ¥2,858,000,000 cut by the end of the month this figure had
been contracted to vC,x Do,000,000. However, the money rates
entinued low and, moreover, a shorp decline in capital invest-
ments in business enterprise was netud. This was attributed
partly to the enforcoment of strict cepital control measures
ind partly to the growing lack of materiels for processing in
Jap:n。

=== Page 34 === (confidence 0.99)
Japan. At the same time prices in Japan, both wholesale and
retail, showed a further rise, bringing the price level to
approximately 6 per cent above that of a year ago.
The fureign exchange rate showed a weak undertone but
the rates were maintained within nirrow limits, the yen-dcllar
quotation adhering closely to the 27.00 level. The total
foreign trade for the month of January, according to the figures
released, amounted to ¥381,000,000. This represented a slight
decrease compared with the corresponding mcnth of 1938.
There
was an excess of imports over exports during the month of
¥36,000,000.
With a view to providing larger funds for industrial
expansion, the Ministry of Finance will submit to the Diet a
bill authorizing the doubling of the capital of the Industrial
Bank of Japan, which is now ¥ 50,000,000, enabling it to issue
some ¥ 1,000,000,000 new debentures. The same Ministry also
proposed that, in order to make up the curtailment in the Bank
of Japan's note issue, -- which curtailment amounted to
$ 300,000,000 and was brought about by the export of gold
reserves, -- another fiduciary note issue of from ¥ 300,000,000
to ¥ 500,000,000 be permitted beyond the  1,700,000,000 previous-
ly authorized.
The trend toward intensified economic control in the
country and centralization and regimentation of industry was
further emphasized curin the month.
In this connection a
number of ordinances invoking portions of the National General
obilization Law were approved and a portion of article 21
f this Law was enfcrced by an ordinance promulgated on Janu-
ry 7, which provided that all skilled laborers must register
heir vocational abilities.
Gasoline consumption was further
curtailed.

April 5, 1939.
(Pages 26-29)
Tokyo's monthly report for February.
The first eight pages recount the Embassy's
approaches to the Foreign Office in regard to
(a) the financial situation at Chefoo, (b)
action of the Chefoo Harbor Office, (c) Ameri-
cans at Kuling, (d) restrictions imposed by
Japanese authorities at Tientsin, (e) restric-
tions on trade on the Yangtze, (f) a pass for
a representative of Anderson, Meyer, and
Company, (g) the unsatisfactory reply of the
Japanese to Ambassador Grew's previous approach
in regard to the Customs at Canton, (h) io cases
of violation of American property rights and
interests in China, (i) Japanese intention in
oooupying Hainan Island, (j) Japanese demands
in respeot to the Shanghai Municipal Council.
All of these matters have already been
reported to the Department in separate tele-
grams or despatohes.
In pages 8 to 10 is described Japanese
interest in (a) America's armament program,
(b) the Guam project, and (o) Mr. Grew's
representations in regard to Hainan Island.
The information in regard to the first two
points has previously been received in the
Department. In respect to the representations
in regard to Hainan, the Japanese press
unanimously

=== Page 43 === (confidence 0.99)

only for the purpose of taking concerted action
with Great Britain.

reaction to the terrorist activities at
Shanghai. This reaotion is already known to
the Department. Ambassador Grew's representa-
tions received no publioity; British representa-
tions were given wide publicity.
On page 12 is desoribed the occupation of
Hainan and its significance. The material is
not new.
The information on pages 13 and 14 in
regard to hostilities in China is not new.
In regard to Anglo-Japanese relations
(pages 14-17), it is stated that although no
marked change was shown in February, the
general sentiment in Japan of antagonism and
antipathy toward Great Britain was acoentuated
by continued differences.
In regard to Japanese-Soviet relations in
the fisheries question, it is stated that in
the House of Peers it was suggested (page 19)
that, in order to meet the strength of the
state-managed fisheries of Soviet Russia, all
Japanese fisheries in northern waters should
be under state management. It was stated that
the Government is in fact considering "a national
polioy company.

neotion with the China Incident, save where

the Navy considers there is no particular need
for establishing a naval base on the continent
and that "for the present, therefore, the Navy
has not intentions of establishing such a base
in any part of China".

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Representations made by the Embassy Regarding
Japanese Activities Interfering with or Restrict
ing American Rights and Interests in China.
During the month of February, the Embassy mude, in
conformity with the Department's instructions, representations
to the Foreign Office regarding a number of specific incidents
of Japanese interference with and restrictions of American
rights and interests in Chinn.
On February 6, two notes were addressed to the Foreign
Minister in regard to recent actions taken by the Japanese navel
authorities at Chefoo. The one note* referred to the statement
of the Commander of the Japanese Landing Forces at Chefoo
that, effective January 15, 1939, export and import permits
issued by the Japanese naval authorities at Chefoo would be
required, that bills of exchange covering the merchandise must
be negotinted with the Yokohama Specie Bank at the "pegged"
r-te of exchange of the Federal Reserve Bank currency and that
the Japanese naval authorities on February 1 began refusing all
permits unless bills of exchange were sold to the Yokohama Specic
Bank at the "pogged" rate. The note then protested to the Japan-
esc Government against the requirement of these import and ex-
port permits nd also with respect to the elleged formation of
the guilds organized by certnin Japanese and Chinese merchants
to obtoin special privileges from the Japanese naval authorities
at Chefoo, on the grounds that these  ctivities constitute an
unwarranted interference with Americnn trade in China. The
ther note* cited the announcement of the Chofoo Harbor Office,

II. RELATIONS WITH OTIHER COUNTRIES.
(a) China.
(1). Terroristic Activities in Shanghai.
The situation that developed during the month at Shanghai
793.94
over the terroristic acts in the International Settlement was
given full publicity in Japun. A demand was made in the Diet
that the Japanese Government employ all practical means, incining
those of force if necessary, to ensure the maintenance of peace
and order in the Settlement. Both the Foreign Minister and the
War Minister made official statements regarding the subject.
The former expressed the opinion that the terroristic acts in-
volved the right of self-defense, and that the Japanese Govern-
ment has in mind plans to deal with the situation both at the
time being and from a more permanent point of view; but, he
explained, the right of self-defense would not be prematurely in-
voked. The War Minister stated that so long as the .unicipal Coun-
cil was unable to guarantee the safety of the lives of Japanese
natioials and pro-Japanese Chinese in the Settlemerts, the
Japanese Governnent could not rely on the present policing of the
Settlement, and that if such a sitution continued there would
be no help but for Japan to take appropriate and effective
measures of self-defense.

(2). Bombing of Hong Kong Territory by Japanese
Planes on February 21.
Upon receipt of a report from the Governor General of
Hong Kong of the bombing which occurred on February 2l by Japan-
ese planes of Hong Kong territory near the Cuinese frontier at
Shumchun, the British Ambassador made a strong protest to the
Foreign Minister against this attack on British territory. A
brief announcement by the War Department was made concerning
the incident in which the incident was described as an accident
that involved one pilot who mistook Hong Kong territory for
Cuinese territory and dropped two bombs on a railway train. On
February 22 the Foreign Minister made to the British Ambassador
an expression of regret for the incident and gave an undertaking
to pay indemnification for the damage to life and property as
well as an assurance that the recurrence of such an incident
would be prevented. The British Eubassy, on Feurualy 28,
informed the Japanese Foreign Office that the British Government
regarded the incident as having been closed on these lines.*

(c) France.
(1). French Representation Concerning the. French
Concession at Tientsin.
On January 30, the French Ambassador addressed a note
to the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs in regard to Japanese
actions concerning the French Concession at Tientsin. The note
referred to the measures taken by the local Japanese authorities
at Tientsin which in effect tended to isolate the Concession.
After stating that the French authorities in the Concession
were doing everything possible to maintain its neutrality and
to prevent disturbances from occurring inside its confines, the
note requested that steps be taken by the Japanese Government to
terminate the Japanese measures.**

(2). French Representations Regarding
Hainan Island.
In its international aspects, the Japanese occupation or
Hainan Island is undoubtedly of concern to the French colony of
Indo-China. While under Chinese sovereignty the Island had re-
mained undeveloped and practically unfortified, and, therefore,
its military and strategical significance to France was negligibie
However, should it now be built up by the Japanese as a military
stronghold, due to its geographical location, it might well con-
stitute a major threat to the security of Indo-China. It was
therefore to be expected that France had to take official cog-
nizance of the military occupation of this Island and on Febru-
ary 13 the French Ambassador called on the Minister for Foreign
Affairs and inquired as to the purpose, the character, and dura-
tion of the occupation. He was informed, in reply, that the pur-
pose of the occupation was to intensify the blockade of the South
China Coast, thet the character of the occupation was military,
and that the length of the occupation would depend upon military
considerations.*
Nothing further in regard to this subject occurred during
the month.

(2) Italo-German-Japanese Anti-Comintern Pact.

III.
INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a) Political Parties.
It was publicly announced on February 9 that two minor
political parties, the Social Mass Party, and the Tohokai, would
merge to form a single national totalitarian party. The Social
Mass Party, which is led by Iso Abe and has 35 representatives
in the Lower House, is considered - in a comparative sense -
the extreme left party of the Diet.
Until 1937 its program
was one of social democracy, but in that year its policies were
modified in conformity with the trend in Japan away from liberal
political tendencies and the party turned to a program that was
along lines of totalitarianism. The Tohokai, headed by Seigo
Nakano, is an ultra-nationalist group which has but 12 representa-
tives in the Lower House. A joint manifesto issued by these two
leaders stated thet, lthough the perties had hed different views
in the past, their concern for the welfare of the State end the
people had been the same and that, on the basis of the principle
of Government by no classes and by no factions, had docided to
form a national totaliterinn party. However, it soon appeared
that

=== Page 72 === (confidence 0.98)
23-
this decision by the two parties to merge was not to be imple-
mented. It wns loarnod that the members of the two parties
had not been fully consulted in the matter and were not agreed
upon its desirability; and also it developed that no ngreament
could be arrived nt as to who should lend the proposed new group.
An announcement appeared at the end of the month that the merger
had been abandoned, at least for the time being. Despite the
fact that the two parties concerned in this matter are of relative
minor political importance in Jepan, the question of their merger
aroused a good deal of public interest duc to its possible sig-
nificance in any trend towards the growth of a strong totalitarian
party or movement in Japan.
(b) The Diet.
Both Housos of the Diet were engaged during the month
in considering Government bills. An outline of these bills is
given in the section below under "Course of Legislation". With
the consideration of these bills, there also continued inter-
pellations in co itto meetings ond discussions ensued in the
House of Representotivus and the House of Peers concerning the
Government's foruien nd domestic policies.
The question of reform of Government administration which
hnd first arisen during the tine of th Konoyo C.binet was again
brought up for discussion in the Diet in February. In the Upper
House, Baron Kimmochi Okure is ruported to have declared that the
Govcrnment officinls are seeking from the nation cooperetion for
the Government's policies merely by issuing orders, that coopera-
tion of the people with the Government cannot be effective when
it is forced upon them in this manner, that as far as the people
are

=== Page 73 === (confidence 0.99)
-24-
are concerned Japan has na-ional solidarity but that there is a
necessity of more solidarity in the Government itself, where the
officials are too much concerned in struggling for authority ena
in fostering the ends of their perticular departments. The Prime
Minister replied to this interpellation that the Goverrment realiz.
that the execution of domestic reforms was urgently necessary and
that full attention would be paid to it but he made no commitment
as to what measures he proposed to adopt to carry out the
"necessary reforms". The criticism of Baron Okura waw echoed in
a number of editorials in the press which asserted that the public
was indignant over what it felt was a self-interested attitude on
the part of the Government bureaucrats, maintained that many of th.
present officials were inefficient, and that their concepts of
business management and administration were incompetent, and re-
quested that the Government should now endeavor to engage able
men for official positions.

(c) Important Statements by Government Cfficials.
Besides the statements by Government officials referred
to in the foregoing sections of this report, the following importar
statements were made during the month under review by Covernment
Ministers before the Diet:
The Foreign Minister in reply, on February 1, to an in-
terpellation in the Budget Committee of the House of Representetive
is reported to have stated that Japan would not, as a general prin-
ciple

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.99)

ciple, make good the losses suffered by nationals of third Powers

The Navy Minister in reply, on February 8, to an inter-

is reported to have stated that he felt that there was need, in
carrying out reforms in the economic structure of the country, for
a limit of controlled economy, that control over everything from

On the same day, the Navy Minister, in reply to another

tives, stated that the Navy considers that there is no particular
need for establishing n naval base on the continent and that "for
the present, therefore, the Navy has no intentions of establishing
such a bese in any part of China".

more important measures also came up for debate during February:
the Health Control Bill to provido stricter control for the preven-
tion of social diseases; four shipping bills to assist the financir:
of shipbuilding end to regulate and control tho Japanese shipping
industry; the Steel Import Bill, to provide for the importation
free of duty of various clesses of steel and iron ond their product:
an insurance bill; five bills relating to arbitration, to permit
various kinds of pursonal disputes to be settled by arbitration
instead of in the courts; a religious bill* to define the status
of religious organizations end to permit Govornment control of
such organizations; a military sucrets bill, to suppress and
prevent industrial espionage,** end the Rice Distribution Control
Bill, to plnco the distribution of rice under Government control,
to assure adequate supplies'of rice for tho nation and to curb
speculation in that commodity. In regard to this last cited bill
it is noted that the proposed measures providing for a virtual
national monopolization of the distribution of rice brought forth
n storm of protest from rice dealers and officinls of rice exchenges
in ell parts of Japan. Manifestos were issued by the rice dealers!
associntions and dclegations came to Tokyo nd held public meet-
ings in demonstration agninst the measure.
V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
Only one lot of Government bonds, amounting to
Yen 300,000,000, was: issued and floated during the month. This
issue brought the national debt up to almost Yen 17,000,000,000.
Figures
* Embassy's despatch No. 3722, March 1, 1939.
**Embassy's despatch No. 3714, March 1, 1939.

Figures were issued during the month regarding the amoun-
of savings by the nation for the first nine months of the current
fiscal year, i.e., from April 1, 1938 to December 1, 1938.
figures announced by the Finance Ministry for this period were
Yen 5,665,000,000, which amount was said to be 70 per cent of
the goal of Yen e,000,000,000 set for national thrift, for int
current fiscal year.
Some interesting statistics were also released during fe)-
ruary regarding the increase in taxation in Japan in recent yenrs
According to the figures submitted by the Finance Ministry to the
House of Representatives, per capita internal taxes in the country
had been doubled during the past four years. In 1935 the per
capita tax was Yen 11.24 and in 1938 Yen 22.64. The following
table was published in the press showing the increase both in

VI. GENERAL ECONCMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITICNS.*
Sentiment in general business circles continued depressed
during the month, mainly due to the rigid control measures which
have been affecting practically every phase of business activity.
The various Government bills announced as cortemplated or sub-
mitted to the Diet in February further intensifying state control
were said to be adding to the difficulties not only of foreign but
also of domestic trade.
One of the outstanding developments during the month was
the very sharp rise in raw silk prices which by the last days of
February
This section propared with the assistance of the Commercial
Attaché's Office.

=== Page 77 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
February had soared to above Yen 1,Oo0 per bale. This situation
was brought about not by demand from abroad, but partly by
unprecedented requirements from domestic quarters, partly by
curtailmenc of supply in Japan and partly also by speculation.
Since cotton textiles have been prohibited for domestic consumptio.
Japanese families have been resorting to silk goods which are free
from state restrictions. Last year's silk production was approxi-
mately 20 per cent below that of 1937. With this sharp decline in
output, coupled with a continued fair demand from ebroad, the
marked increase in local consumption and the paucity of stocks,
a rise in market price became inevitable but was as well accen-
tuated by speculators who were predicting an even more stringent
situation in the months to come. Prices of other commodities also
strengthened nnd it was announced that the Government, becoming
apprehensive of the general tendency for rising prices in the
country, bad decided to t ke steps to curb this tendency. It
was reported that the present Central Price Policy Commission would
be reorganized, that Mr. Seihin Ikeda, former Finance Minister,
was to become its new chairman, and that it would immediately
proceed with steps to devise and put into operation an effective
price control system. Following these announcements it was re-
ported that the textile in ustry had decided voluntar'ly to reduce
the price of many textile articles, and retail prices were reported-
ly accordingly cut on such articles from 15 to 30 per cent.
Thd stock market developed & more encouraging turn around
the middle of February and quotations showed a modurete but steady
upward tendency. This was largely due to current higher commodity
prices. However, towards the end of the month with the Government
attempts

=== Page 78 === (confidence 0.98)
-23-
attempts to check this price advance the stock quotations tended to
weaken. Money rates were easy throughout the month and a large supp:
of funds availeble for investment. It was announced by the Bank of J.
that a tota1 sum of Yen 279,000,000 was either invested or called for
by business establishments.
Exchange rates showed little ch ne, fluctuations in the yen-
dollar quotations being confined to narrow limits and adhering close-
1y to the 2725 level.
Statistics released for the foreign trade for the first two montl
showed an excess of imports for the combined trade of January and Feb-
ruary of Yen 69,000,000. Tota1 trad3 for these two months was Yen
110,000,000 above th:t for the corresponding two months of 1938, the
figures nmounting to Yen 882,000,000 nd Yen 774,000,000 respectively.
On Februrry 4, Mr. Hajime Mitsushima, Chief of the Trade Bureau o
the Foreign Ministry, in reply to n interpell tion in the Budget Com-
mittee of the House of Represontotives, is ruported to have announced
that Japen in 1938 had concluded trade agreements with Turkey, It ly,
Poland nnd Australie nd is now nogoti ting for similar pacts with
Germany, France, Finlend, Vunezuel, Argentina, Prraguay nd Uruguey.
These compensatory or barter greemonts conform to ihe Materinl Resour
Mobilization Plan, by which Jpon's foreign trode is restricted and
controlled.

ACTIVITIES OF ALERICAN CCMMERCIAL INTERESTS.

VIII. ACTIVITIES OF FOREIGN COMMERCIAL INTRESTS.

IX.
LABOR.
There appeared a notice in the local press on February 16
concerning a proposed ordinonce shortly to be promulgated by the
Welfare Ministry which would limit the hours of work of workers in
the mechanical and metallurgical industries to 12 hours a day. It
vos estimated that bout a million workers would be ffected by this
ordinance.

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.99)
-30-
ordinance.
In the press articles reporting this proposed ordinance,
data weregivenboncerning the present hours of labor now in effe
in the industries concerned. It was stated that due to the
pressure of the demand for munitions and war materials, many
factories are understood to be keeping workers for 15, 16 and 
and, even in some instances,20 hours a day.
The projected ordinance reportedly provides that workers
may not be employed for longer than 12 hours a day, except under
special circumstances, the working day may be extended to l4 hours
although this may not happen more than seven days each month, and
that there are to be at least two rest days each month.

May 5, 1939.
Tokyo's monthly report for March 1939.
Pages 1-19 report American-Japanese rela-
tions and British-Japanese relations, largely
being a recital of representations made to the
Foreign Office by American and British Ambas-
sadors. All of the information contained in
these pages has been received previously in
the Department in despatch or telegraph form.
Pages 19-27 deal with Japan's relations
with Ohina, France, and other countries.
These pages summarize information which had
already reached the Department through other
channels.
The remainder of the despatch deals with
Japan's internal conditions and does not con-
tain fresh significant information.
Thi monthly report of the Embassy at
Tokyo is evidently not supplied to the follow-
ing offices: Consulate General at Tokyo and
Consulates at Yokohama, Nagoya, Nagasaki, and
Taihoku. Without risking sending the report
in the open mail copies could be delivered to
the first three consular offices named and it
would probably be safe enough to send copies
to the Consulates at Nagasaki and Taihoku in
"envelock" envelopes. In view of the great
difficulty in obtaining through the press in
Japan  accurate information with regard to/
developments

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
developments in Japan's external and internal
situation, I think this report should be made
available to as many American Foreign Service
officers in Japan as possible. With this re-
port to keep the officers au courant, the of-
ficers at the posts mentioned above might at
times be able to supply the Embassy with
supplementary information.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STAT.
(a) North China Trede Restrictions
In a telegran dated Karch  the Department, referring
711.94
reports received from the American mbassy at Peiping and the Amei
can Consulates in North China in rognd to trade rostrictions j.
North China, instructed the Aml esr to approach the Foreign of-
fice in such a manner as he light deem appropriate and to make
representations concerning the drastic trade restrictions, the im-
position of which the Government of the United States was informed
was contemplated by the Japanese sponsored régime in North China.*
It will be recalled that the position of the United States
in regard to Japanese interference with and restriction of American
rights and interests in China on general as contrasted to specific
lines had been comprehensively dealt with in the United States Gov.
ernment's notes to the Japanese Governmert of October 6 and Decem.
ber 30, 1938, in which there were laid down the United States Gover
ment's reservations with recard to such rights and interests. It
was felt that there were arguments against initiating a new cycle
of formal reservations, refusal by the Japanese authorities and re-
newed laying down of reservations with the attendant undesirable
and unprofitable ncrimony and irritation and that more might be
gained by making frequent informal approuches. With this thought in
mind the desirability of presenting informally to the Foreign
Office an aide mémoire on the subject of trade restrictions and
exchange control in North China was considered. However, in view
of the fact that the British and French mbassadors had been in-
structed by their respective governments to make formal representa-
tions, and in view of the question raised by the Department of the
desirability
* Embassy's telegram No. 52, March 4, 1939.

=== Page 95 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
desirability of concerted action wherever practicable, it was decid.
ed to present a formal note to the Ministry of Foreign Affairs.*
In tiis note, presonted on March ll,** the renewed atten
tion of the Foreign Minister wes invited to the Ambassador's not
to the Forsign Office of February 6, 1939, rulating to the impositic
by the Japanese naval authorities at Chefoo of various restrictions
on the shipment of merchandise, to the Embassy's informal memorendun.
left with tho Foreign Office on Februnry 6 relnting to unwarranted
restrictions placed upon American personnl and business interests
in Tientsin, and to the nide mémoire which wns left at the Foreign
Office on March 8 in which the hope was expressed that the necessary
steps would immediately be taken to alleviate the continued im-
position of the rostrictions at Tientsin. It was then st ted
thnt the Govurnment of the United States regarded the new drastic
trade restrictions, including the requircment of export permits
and controlled money exchange, imposed by the Japanese sponsored
régime in North China with the support of Japanese authorities,
as the most comprehensive discriminction against the United States
and other foreign countries and in favor of Japan which has yet
been established in North China by Japanese authorities and as a
virtual nullification in that area of the principle of equal
opportunity o far as imports end exports are concerned. The cf-
fect of these mensures in impairing the trade with North China of
foreign countries other than Japan while at the same time bene-
fitting the t rade of Japan to the prejudice of other foreign inter-

can Government has repeatedly advanced to the institution of trade

(1) Anglo-Chinese Agreement to Establish a
Chinese Currency Stabilization Fund.
On March 8* the British Ambassador called on the Vice Minister
for Foreign Affnirs end informed him of the agreement that would be
announced
Embassy's telegrem No. 122, March 8, 1939.

=== Page 106 === (confidence 0.98)
13.
mounced batween the British Government and the Chinese National
Government for the ostablishment of & Chinese currency stabilizatio
fund, leaving with him a memorandum outlining the plan for the
ostablishment of the fund. At the snme time Sir Robert Craigie
informed the Vice Minister that the agreement was not to he re-
garded as a measure to support the Chiang Kai-shek régime but
purely and simply as an action in defense of British interusts in
China.
In substance, the agreement provided that the Chinese Govern-
ment Banks operating in China form & currency stabilization fund
of 10 million pounds to be usod for exchange operations to prevent
undue fluctuation in the value of the Chinese national dollar. The
two British banks operating in China, the Hong Kong and Shanghai
Banking Corporation, and the Chartored Bank, were to subscribe five
million pounds and the Bank of China and Bank of Communications
were to subscribe the other five million pounds. The fund was to
be maneged by a committee in Hong Kong, the agreement to remain
in force for a year, the British Government undertaking to reim-
burse the British banks for any losses that might be incurred by
them.
In a later conversation on the same day between Sir Robert
nd Vice Minister Sawada, the latter stated that the Japanese
Government questioned, in regard to the agreement, whether there
as any immedinte danger of a collapse of the Chinese currency which
would necessitate the establishment of the fund, and that, becnuse of
the lerge amount of the fund, the Japanese Government entertained
coubt whether it was to be used solely for mnintaining the stability
of the currency. In reply to theso questions, Sir Robert informed
r. Sawada that no such undertaking would have been entered upon.
f there had not been a real need for it, and that the fund was
only

=== Page 107 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
only to be used for the purpose stated, its amount having to be su-
ficiently large to obviate all possibility of speculative attack.
Full publicity was given to the agreement and to the conver-
sation between the British Ambassador and the Vice Minister for
Foreign Affairs concerning the British intentions in establishing
the fund.
The matter was taken up in the Diet and in reply to inter-
pellations the Foreign Minister is reported to have stated in effect
that the fund would indirectly operate to be helpful to the Chiang
Kai-shek régime and would, therefore, have substantial effect on
Anglo-Japanese relations, but that at the same time inasmuch as it
could admittedly be, as Great Britain had assured Japan, for the
protection of Britain's own rights and interests, Japan must
watch how the fund would work out and see whether the establishment
of such a fund warranted a protest.
In direct variance with the contentions of the Foreign Minister
as expressed in his message on March Eth through the Vice Minister
for Foreign Affairs to the British Ambassador, that there did not
appear to be any immediate danger of a collapse of the Chinese
currency, a spokesman of the Foreign Office declared that he believed
that the Chinese national currency stood on the verge of complete
collapse. This fact, he said, undoubtedly accounted for the new
British scheme, as the British Government had made it known that
the stabilization of the Chinese national currency would be a great
help to British enterprise and trade in China. He asserted, however,
that England should realize the "trend of affairs in East Asia" and
cather than support the old Chinese national currency should help
'stabilize the new currency" - presumably that of North China.
He

=== Page 108 === (confidence 0.99)
then concluded that the real motive berind the British aer was
to give financial and economic aid to the Chieng Kai-shek régime
in order to restore the status quo ente of Enst Asia but that sucn
a policy was irimical to the"trend of affairs".
Editorial comment corcerning the Anglo-Chinese agroorens sppver-
ed in a series of articles in all the newspapers and a strong advers?
reaction, manifested from the start, soon developed into an openlv
hostile and antagonistic attitude toward Great Britain over the
agreement. Following the line taken in the Foreign Office's
statement, there was expressed a general refusal to believe the
British assuranccs that the measure was to be of a purely economic
character designed to protect British interests, and it was held
thet the undertaking was a political one for the principal purpose
of supporting the Chinese Government. It was further charged that
there was no doubt that it was aimed at bringing economic pressure
to bear on Japan.
The stand was taken that the Government should
desist from a "wait and see" policy and not put up with "Great
Britain's malicious intont towerds Japan".
Amidst this storm of protest over the Anglo-Chinese agreement
there nevertheless inconspicuously appeared in editorials of
certain sections of the press, particularly those of a financial and
commercial nature, comments to the effect that Japan might do well not
to combat the British move but itself to utilize the present stabil-
ized currency for its own ends. It was pointed out that Japan would
experience difficulties in trying to cstablish un entirely new cur-
rency system in Central and South China, thit the present currency
had been maintained at its current value of eight pence because of
the support given it by the British and American banks, and that in
tilizing the currency Japan could benefit its industry and trade with
heareas mentioned.

and support of the Japanese Government conflicted with the assur-

rights of third Powers in Chinn would be respected.

nent reserved the right to toko ony action that might seem approprint

2. Military Operations.
The first mejor militory operations which had occurred
since the termination of the Japanese campaigns gainst
Hankow and Canton last fall were begun in the middle of the
month in large scale drives on Nancheng and Nuning in northern
Kiangsi Province. On the Siao River front the Japanese drive
opened on March 20 against Wuning. Two days later the Japancse
drive southward elong the reilway to Nanchong was storted.
On
March 27 the Japanese forces occupied Nanchang and on March 29
Nuning was also reported to have been captured. It was under-
stood that in both these campnigns, particularly that on Wuning
where the Chinese had put up a very strong resistance, heavy
fighting between the contending forces took place.
During the last few dnys of the month it was also reported
that the Japanese hnd launched new military operations in the Cont
area in South China and thet Kongmoon on the west bank of the
Pearl River delta was occupied on March 30.
In the earlior part of the month a Japaneso campaign to
the north nd northwest of Hankow towards the Han River was under-
taken and it was reported th:t by the middle of the month Japanuse
forces had captured Kiukow end Anlu on the Han River thus extendir
a salient to within less than loo miles from Ichang to the west
and

and less than 80 miles from Siangyang to the north. Roports
wero also received thot fighting occurred betwoen Japanese uni.

the northwest of Taiyuan and captured Tsinglo; in Hopei the
campaign nginst tho guerrillas in the central part of that
Province was understood to have been completed and the Chinese
guerrilla leaders believed to have fled to Shensi; in Kiangsu,
Hwaiyan on the Grand Canal nd Haichow at the old mouth of the
Yellow River were captured, while Fowning on the Shehyang River
was also occupied. Bombing by Japanese military and naval
units continued throug'.out the month and raids were made on a
number of centers in Shensi Province, on Lanchov, Kansu Province, on
Chengchow and other towns in north Honan nd along the Chekiang-
Kiangsi railway and on various towns in Kiangsi.
Considerable significance was given to the capture of
Nanchang and Vuning in Japan. It is understood thot the objective
of the campaign was in a largc part to cut the supply route from
west China to the castern provinces of Kiangsi and Chekiang and
that with the fall of Nanchang ot the hend of the Chekiang-
Kiangsi Reilway, which railway extends to Hsioshan on Hongchow
Bay, the Japanese heve effectively disrupted this route. *
From en economic point of view the occupation of Nanchong
was also held to be very valuable inasmuch as the province of
Kiangsi is understood to produco cpproximatcly e0 per cent of
tungsten ore found in Chin. As Chinn produces about 90 per
cent of the world's supply of tungsten Japan is held to have
thereby guined control of this product which forms an essential
alloy in the manufacture of many high-grade metal products.

(d) oviet Russia.
Although negotiations were continued during the month under
review between Japan and Soviet Russia over the Siberian fisher-
ies question, no solution of this problem had been reached by the
end of the month. It was reported in the press that the previous-
ly announced auction to be held at Vladivostok on March 15 by the
Soviet Government of the rights to the fishery grounds in Soviet
Far Eastern Waters, took place on that date as scheduled.
Under
a decision taken by the Japanese Government and Japanese fishing
interests no Japanese attended the auction. However, it was later
learned that the Russian fishery concerns had bought at the auction
the rights for only four lots out of the 29s taken up in past
years by Japanese fishing concerns and from this an optimistic view
was assumed that the door had not been entirely closed to the
eventual amicable settlement of the question. In the meanwhile
it was announced that Japan would continue to press for an agree-
ment with Russia over the fisheries until the last moment before
the

(e) Italy.
1. Japan-Italo Cultural Agreement.
Concluding negotietions that had been going on since last
December, on March 23 there was signed at Tokyo between the
Foreign Minister, Mr. Hachiro Arita, and the Italian mbassedor,
Mr. Giacinto Auriti, an agreement for Italo-Japanese cultural
cooperation. The agreement is similar to thet concluded between
Japan and Germany last November. The provisions of the instru-
ment, set forth in a few short articles, are that Japan and Italy
should strive by coll-boration to further their cultural relations
and that they should promote their cultural relations through the
medium of science, art, music, literature, stcge, screen, photo-
graphy, radio, youth movements, sports, otc. The general prin-
ciples, it was concurrently nnounced by the Foreign Office, would
be implemented by the foundntion of cultural institutions and com-
mittoes cnd by the exchange of professors, students, text books,
works of art, films, etc. A st tement concerning the agreement
issued by a spokosman of the Forcign Office was to the effect
that the Japanese Government hnd initiated the negotietions with
Italy with the objective in view of cooperating in cultural under-
standing with those foreign countries which are in friendly rela-

(f) General International Relations.
1. Spratley Islands.
It was officially announced on March 31 by the Japanese
Government that the Spretley Islands, or as they are termed in
Japanese "Sinnan Gunto", hod been plrced as of March 30 under
the jurisdiction of the Government General of Formosa. The
islands comprise smrll coral reefs in the South China Sen at a
point bout 300 milus southeast of French Indo-China, 340
miles northwest of British Borneo and 37o miles west of the
western extremity of the Philippine Islands.
In a statement issued by the Foreign Office it was claimed
that the islands were long considered to be ownerless but that
in l9l7 Japan begen, before the nationals of any other country,
to embark upon the economic development of the islands, that this
development had continued since then, and that a considerable
amount of capital had been invested for the exploitation of the
deposits of phosphate on them nd for the construction of various
permanent establishments. Although the islands are believed to
be too small to be of any appreci:ble economic value a stretegic
importnnce is attributed to their possession. As they lie almost
in the center of the South Chinn Sen and are not far distant from
Indo-Chin:, the Enst Indies, the southern end of the Philippine
Islands end Singapore, they afford a position of observation of
aerinl and neval movements to and from those places.

=== Page 120 === (confidence 0.99)
2.
Statement by the Prime Minister in Regard to
Japan's General Foreign Policy.
In an interview with newspaper reporters on March 29, the
Prime Minister is reported to have stated that "it would appear
that totalitarianism and liberalism are the burning questions
of the day. However, it is not in the 'spirit of Japan for
Japan to associate itself with liberal or totalitarian blocs
which would oppose each other".
This statement was no doubt an
allusion to the recent political developments that had occurred
in Europe and to the agitation in certain Japanese quarters for
the strengthening of the anti-Comintern Pact.
III.
INTERNAL POLITICAL CONDITICNS.
(a) The Diet.
The 74th session of the Diet, named by the vernacular
press "The Asia Promotion Diet", was adjourned on March 26, the
date scheduled for its closing. During the session, lasting
just three months, all the measures introduced by the Government
were enacted without serious protest and without any major altera-
tions. The 14 budget measures were passed in their original form
as were 78 of the 89 legislative bills. Only minor changes in the
other legislative bills were made and in every case with the
assent of the Government. No bills proposed from the floor of the
Diet were passed although some 228 petitions were adopted and sent
to the Government.*
Besides the attention given to consideration and en-
actment of the Government bills, many of which were hurriedly
pressed through in the last days of the session in order that
legislation might be completed before the date set for adjournment.

comnleted without considering the future demends with which the
nation would likely be faced in the program for the reconstruc-

(a) Return of the Ashes of Ambassador Saito to Japan
by the U.S.S. ASTORIA.
On April 17 the U.S.S. ASTORIA arrived at Yokohama bearing
to Japan at the President's behest the ashes of the late
Japanese Ambassador to Washington, Hiroshi Saito, and remainec
as
in Japan on the ceremonial visit,/previously arranged, for
nine days. The delivery of the ashes by Captain Richmond K.
Turner of the ASTORIA to Mr. Sawada, the Vice Minister for For-
eign Affairs as Chairman of the Reception Committee, the cere-
701.9411

remains to Tokyo and the funeral services in the Buddhist Honganji
Temple were carried cut with marked solemnity, dignity and im-
pressiveness. The remaining days of the visit of the ASTORIA in
Japan were given ovor to the official reception and entertainment
of the personnel of the ASTORIA, with calls by Captain Turner on
the Government Ministers, dinners by Ambassador Grew and the For-
cign and Navy Ministers, and by tho House of Pcers and Represen-
tatives end a tea at the Imporial Gardens. On April 24 an
audience was arranged on Japanese initintive for the presentation
of Captcin Turner to the Emperor, and on that occasion the Emperor
requested the Ambassador to convey to the President his message
of gratefulness to the President and the Government and people
of the United States for their sympathy shown on the death of
Ambassador Saito and particularly for the President's courtesy
in providing a warship for trensporting the cshes to Japan.
On the same dy, to express the gratitude of the Japanese
poople to the United States Government and puople, a "Wolcome

held under the joint auspices of verious privete Japanese orgeni-

closely associted with communiction problems for m ny yers, hav-
ing served in the Ministry of Communications in various capacities
s ince 1905. He was called from retirement in 1932 to the post of
Privy Councillor of "Manchukuo", which post he held until 1937,
and he was prominently mentioned for the post of Minister of Com
munications in that ye r. The selection of Mr. Tanabe to head
the Ministry of Communicetions was generally held to be n good
one, as, being in export on communicutions, he would be a capable
person to hondle the incrensingly importent duties of the Ministry,
including the control over the natienal electric power industry,

Jopan's plans for expansien on the Continent.*

(b) Political Parties.
1. Dissension in the Seiyukai over Selection of

between various foctions in the party, centering principally betw

immedintely denounced by th. "orthodox league", and it wns under-

t he two f ctions were now sepor tely claiming to represent the

st te of impotence to which the pertics have been reduced make

significance.

4. Proposed Commodity Price Control Plan.
In consonance with other developments th't have been taking

replace the hophozord meesurus previously t'ken under commodity
control rugulations and nti-profiteering ordinances; to stop

zation of the supply and demends of conmodities end generally to
bolstur up the conomic structurc of the courtry. After long

jectives of price control, the system to be based on stabiliza-

2. Militery Operations.
The principal Japanese military activity during the
month of May was a campaign involving rather large forces,
said to number four or five divisions, in the Han River area
of north central Hupeh, northwest from a line between Sinyang
and Anlu. The operation was evidently made to clear the Tahung
and

=== Page 206 === (confidence 0.98)
-82-
and Tapioh mountain rangos of a concontration of Chinese
forces claimed by tho Japaneso to havo amounted to twenty-six
divisions. Three main drives wero made to encircle the Chinese
army; one up the Han River; one west from Sinyang on tho Peip-
ing-Hankow Railway; and another uriving northwost between the
first two. Those three columns were reportod to have con-
verged by the middle of the month noer Sisintsi in the plains
north of the Tapieh Mounteins. Operations were then continued
in rounding up Chinese units in the erea thet had been on-
circled and in the capturing of the towns of Tseoyang and
Suihsien. Although the Japanese claimed that the Chinese
forces had suffered some 15,o00 cesualties, it eppeared that
the mein bodies of the Chinese troops managed to escape through
tho Japanese cordon to the west.
Further fighting was reported to have occurred in north-
crn, central end southern Shansi and to have continued through-
out the month. In the Wutaishen Mountains in central Shansi,
an opcration was roportodly taken against an estimated 30,000
Chinese troops, but little was rovealed as to the result of
this campaign; in southern Shansi various campaigns were
launched which apparently by tho end of the month had still
not succeeded in cleering the region of Chinese troops.
Furthor fighting against regular Chineso forces report-
edly occurred south and west of Henkow and around Nenchang, whi

Japanese aerial operations were very intensively car-

Tokyo's monthly report for July 1939
contains no information of importance which
has not already reached the Division in
telegram or despatch form.
You will be interested in the report
that a good-will mission of Thai journalists
visited Japan in the month of July, receiving
much public attention; that Japan has decided
to open a legation t Iraq; and that Japan
has decided to establish a legation in Bul-
garia.
James Espy has been writing the Tokyo
monthly report for some mon ths and I think he
does them extremely well. They are well
written; a great deal of thought and care
evidently enter into the preparation; and
they give a complete picture of developments
each month which 1s of especial value, I find,
for reference purposes. I you have no
objection, I should like to draft an instruction
commending Mr. Espy on the preparation of the
current monthly report.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Fur Trade Restrictions in North China.
In conformity with the Department's telegraphic instruc
tions* Mr. Dooman on June 16 addressed a note** to the Ministe
for Foreign Affairs protesting against the continued inter-
ference by Japanese authorities in North China with Amerioan
trade there in furs and skins. There was first pointed out
in the note the American interest in North China in these
products. This interest was defined as threefold: (1) America.
firms were prominent in the business of purchasing and ex-
porting furs and skins from North China; (a) American con-
sumers take the bulk of Chinese exports of domestic animal
furs and skins and are to an important extent dependent
upon Chinq as a sonrce of supply; and (3) the curtailment of
exports from North China to the United Statos, or to other
free currency areas, necessarily reduced the foreign exchange
available for the purchase of American products. Note was
then made of tho unwarranted interferonce since the occupa-
tion of the area by Japanese military forces to which the
fur trade in North China had been subjected almost contin-
uously by agencies of the Japanesc Government.
After giv-
ing a description of the actions in this connection on the part
of the authorities in North China and making reforence to
previous representations made by tho American Government in
tho matter, it was stated that it appeared that the principal
interference was caused by the Japanese military, who had
claimed that such action was warranted by military necessity.
This

**Embassy's telegram No. 275, June 16, and despatch

The Ministry of Finance issued on June 13 China Incidont Bonds

A substantially large issue was disposed of to the public through

In a speech mede by the Parliamentary Finance Vice Minister
on June 15, he is reported to have stated in part that the Govern-
ment had spent Yen 12,000,000 since the beginning of the "China
Incident" to carry on the hostilities up to that date, but that
the aggregate national earnings were estimated at approximately
Yen 80,000,000, and that accordingly only about 40 per cent of the
total national earnings were expended in connection with tho China
Affair. According to his statement also total tax receipts in
the amount of Yen 2,330,000,000 for the present fiscal year were

a reduction of Yen 1.07, was made on June 21. The second was made

States Treasury in regard to its purchase of silver.
VI. GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.*
Further strengthening of state economic control, renowed out-
breaks on the Manchuria-Outer Mongolian borders, the suspension of
official control over the selling of foreign exchange in Shanghai,
and the Japanese blockade of the British and French Concessions in
Tientsin all contributed to a cautious and apprehensive business
sontiment during Juno. New economic control measures enforced
during the month included an ordinanco promulgated on May 27, and
effective the same date, placing the export of major chomical fer-
tilizers under the license system. Effective May 25 tho oxport
of ropes, tape, toys, ctc., containing wool was placed under the
link system allowing the import of one balo of wool for the export
of ll0 pounds of woollen goods. Effective July 1 tho Cotton
Demand Supply Adjustment Council was to assume control of all kinds
of yarns in a swoeping reform plan for the distribution and control
of fibrous materiels. It was also reported during the month that
te Ministry of Commorce and Industry had completed plans for the
establi shment
Propared with the assistance of the Office of the Commercial
Attachó.

manage all sales in Japan of crudo oil, heavy oil, gasolins anl
synthotic oil. The firm would be formod by the unification cf
prosent organizations concorned with the salo of potroloum products
and would have charge over prices, quantitios and distribution of
such products. It was announced toward the end of the month that
the Government had docided to put under direct Government control
the shipping bueiness in Japan. By means of this control the
authorities reportedly intend to link the shipping businoss with
the materials, production expansion program and the commodity mobili-
zation program of Japan. The decision for such control was adopted
instoad of a. joint Government-business council which had been previou
ly proposod as cmothod to rogulete the activities of the shipping
i ndustry.

During the same period the campaign in Japan against
Great Britain which had begun in June increased in vigor
and hostility. Mass moetings were held in all the large
cities of Japan. Anti-British postors were put up in all
conspicuous public places and in Tokyo parades were made
to the British Embassy. Petitions were signed making
extravagant demands for a change of the basic British
policy in the Far East and calling for the withdrawal
of the British from East Asia and the return of her Con-
cessions to China.

benofited the ir enory and the British Government and its

III.
INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a)
Cabinet
An Imperial Ordinance was promulgated on July 4
which provided for the establishment of a new quasi-
official organ to be known as the Asia Promotion Com-
mission. This Commissi on is to be an advisory body to
the Cabinet's Asia Fromotion Board. It is to be composed
of some 50 members under the supervision of the Prime
Minister. Higher Government officials such as officials
of the Asia Promotion Board, Vice Ifinisters from the dif-
ferent Government departments, Cabinet Councillors, and
a number of the more important figures in Japanese pro-
fessional and industrial life are to be appointed to the
Commission. Presumably the Cornission was established
to study and advise upon matters of general policy concern-
ing Japan's relations with China.*

Invocation of Labor Conscription Ordinance
There was promulgated during the month under review
to become effective on July 15 in Japan Proper and from
October 1st in the othor parts of the Japanese Empire an

types of labor for national mobilization purposes. This
Ordinance is based on Article 4 of the National General
Mobilization Act which cmpowers the Government to draft
Imperial subjects for service in general mobilizat on
enterprises and is related to the Ordinance promulgated
in January of this year for the compulsory registration
of the vocational abilities of skilled workors in a wide
category of industries.**
It was announced in the press that the Governiont
estimated that approximately 1,100,000 men and women
would be affected by these Ordinances, to be called upon
to mako up the shortage of labor in important industries
in Japan, "ianchukuo", and China, particularly in the
technical and skillcd labor lines. The wago scales for
the laborers conseripted were announced as Yon 65-70
for graduates of universitios, Yen 50-60 for graduates
