I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(e)
Assaults on Americen Citizens and Anti-
Foreign Campaign In China.
On August 1, 1939 Mr. Dooman called on tho Vice
Minister for Forcign Affairs and in conformity with the
Department's instructions* made oral representalions to
Mr. Sawada in regard to the continued instances of
assault on American citizens in China by the Japanese
military. The report from the Exbassy at Peiping of the
attack on Mr. A. E. S ithberger and Father Daniel J.
Soammell on July 22 was teken up as the latest incident
that had occurrcd in this regard. The wide publicity in
the United States given to the unwerranted actions on the
part of the Japanese military and their accumulative
adverse effect upon American public opinion towards
Japan were pointed out to the Vice Minister. The fact
was also stressud or the possibility of these assaults
being reflective of the anti-foreign propagande campaign
that was current in Clina.**
Again on August 7, Mr. Dooman called on the Director
of the American Bureau of the Foreion Ufrice and took up
with him the situation in Cnina in regard to the anti-
foreign campaign tliere. On this occasion he reviewed
the situation wherein the anti-foreign agitation sponsored
by Japanese agents, although primarily directed against a
third country, was nevertheless creating increasing dangers
to
*
Department's telegram No. 224, July 2E, 5 p.m.
**
Embassy's telegram No. 379, August 1, 6 p.m.
Embassy's despatch No. 4075, August 4.

=== Page 316 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
to American citizens and property. He emphasized the
fact that the u.erican Government could not ignore such
a condition and emphatically urged that the Japanese
Government realise the serious potentialities of the
agitation stirred up by its agents.
It was immediately reported in the press that these
representations had been made to the Foreign Office and
their general purport was given in the newspaper articles.
Later in the month a number of reports appeared in
the press of categorical denials that any movement in
China had developed against the United States or its
citizens. It was also claimed that the rumors of Chinese
attacks and agitation against Americans and their interests
in various localities there were entirelyrunfounded. It
was declared that these rumors were melicious propaganda
which had arisen from certain quarters for the purpose
of alienating Japanese-American relations. It was also
announced that on August 24 the Japanese Ambassador at
Washington had been instructed to make a strong protest
to the United States Government in regard to the mattar.
(b)
Broader Aspects of the Anglo-Japanese Con-
versations Concerning the Situation at Tientsin.
On August 3, the Chergé d'Affaires called on the
Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs and took up with him
under the Department's authorization the question of the
American Government's position with regard to the broader
aspects of the Anglo-Japanese conversations concerning
the situation at Tientsin. Mr. Dooman referred to
previous conversations which he had with Mr. Sawada and
other officials of the Japanese Government since June,
in

=== Page 317 === (confidence 0.98)
●
- 3 -
in which he had indiceted the likelihood of the American
Government being con:crned if the negotiations were o
lead to the adoption cf moasures which would affect
American rights end interests in North China. He recall3c
the note he had taken of te specific question of a cur-
rency being set up in North China which would be for the
use there of the Japanese only. He then pointed out that
the silver and currency problems had now been raised in
the Anglo-Japanese conversations at Tokyo and stated that
the Japanese could not expect the United States to assent
to any action taken by Japan in making illegal the use of
the recognized legal currency of China.*
(c) International Settlement at Kulangsu, Amoy.
During the month under review further conversations
were held by Mr. Dooman with members of the Foreign Office
over the situation at Kulangsu, Amoy. Mr. Dooman pointed
out on several occasions that the American Government, in
its desire of finding a means of solving the problems at
Kulangsu, had already gone more than half way to meet
the Jepanese, but it could not submit to menacing demands
or to pressure of coercive measures, evidence of which
were still to be seen in the Japanese actions at Amoy.
Replies were received that the Japanese Government on its
part was: also extremely anxious to have a solution of the
situation brought about, that note had been taken of the

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a)
Great Britain
1.
Anglo-Japanese Conference Concerning the
Situation at Tientsin.
It will be recalled that on July 22 a statement w:s
issued announcing a general agreement on the first point
of the Anglo-Japenese negotiations. This formula accord-
ing to the Japanese interpretation was to serve as a com-
mon background and general basis for discussion of the
specific issues at the conference. The British Foreign
Office in London announced that on the part of Great
Britain the statement did not mean that there was any
change in British foreign policy as regards the Far East.
On August 4, the Embassy received an informal explanation
from the British Embassy at Tokyo of the British views
as to the significance of the statement. This explanation
was in the form of a declaration of what the statement
did not do, as follows:
It recognized no right only a situation; it did not
lay on the British Government obligations of a neutral
but only provided for impartiality; it did not promise
anything
Embassy's telegram No. 424, August 22, 3 p.m.

=== Page 323 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
anything involving infraction of the British Government's
obligations to third parties; it did not preclude the
British Government from continuing to press for redress
of their greivances; it involved no admission of past
culpability on the part of the British Government; it
did not give carte blanche to the Japanese to decide
what degree of cooperation by the British was necessary
in measures desired to secure security and public order.
It is recalled that in the last week of July
negotiations had begun on specific questions and that the
Japanese put forward in regard to economic matters three
important suggestions regarding:the prohibiting of the
use of Chinese National currency in the Concession; the
removal of the silver stocks from the Concession; and the
control by local Chinese authorities of Chinese banking
institutions, and the circulation of Chineso currency in
the Concessiorn. As no agreoment could be reached on these
questions, on August 3 the discussions were suspended
pending receipt by the British Embassy of further instruc-
tions from its Government.
In the meantime, on August 12, the British Ambassador
made the proposal that the negotiations be reopened on
the question of policing of the Concession, in regard to
which all but the final details had already been worked

proposal, however, the Japanese turned down, insisting,

no agreement could b: eccopted by them on any one.cucsion
without a settlement being reached on all the questiore.
economic as well as police.
On August 1l, it was announced by the British authori-

although

Reports continued to appear in Japan concerning the

favorable event towards the pacification of the Canton
and Kwangtung area. It was noteworthy that his appeal
for a local truce in South China was received in Japan with
approbation.
Considerable interest, particularly amongst finan-
cial circles in Jepan, was manifested in regard to the
rumored scheme of the Chinese National Government to set
up a new currency system which was either to take the
place of or help bolster up the depreciating Chinese
National currency. It was feared that the British were
behind this scheme and would help to establish the new
currency. It was emphatically declared that should Great
Britain appear to support any new currency system J pan
would take no part in it and would moreover look upon the
act as unfriendly to herself.

(c)
Germany
The news of the announcement on August 22 of the con-
clusion of the German-Soviet non-aggression pact came to
Japan as a stunning blow. It was a complete surprise, as
not even the Japanese Government reportedly had any know-
ledge beforehand. When the terms of the agreement were
published general feeling in both official and private
circles throughout Japan became one of resentment and
indignation. It was held that the treaty in effect
violated the anti-Comintern pact, Article 4 of which
provided that Germany and the Soviet Union would not par-
ticipate in any grouping of Powers which either directly
or indirectly aimed against the other contracting party.
This viow was based, according to the general opinion
put forward in the press, that the Soviet Government and
the Comintern were one and the same thing.
An announcement was made on August 26 by the Foreign
Office that the Japanese Ambassador at Berlin had been
instructed strongly to protest to the German Government
against the conclusion of the agreement. It was also
reported that Ambassedor Oshima was notifying the Reich
Government

=== Page 329 === (confidence 0.99)
- 15 -
Government of the Japanese Government's intentions to en
deliberations for th" strengthening between the two natio
of the anti-Comintern agreement.*
The general reaction of the press was one of holding
that Germany had showed bad faith towerds Japan and had
frankly violated the friendship thet hed been fostered
between the two countries. In disgust, the press editorials
called for a clearing away by Japan of any further entangle
ments with foreign countries and the establishment of an
autonomous and independent policy. It should be noted,
however, that there was in some sections the feeling that
despite what Germany had done, it remained that other
countries, notably Britain and France, were Japan's
greatest obstacles in the carrying out of its policy of
establishing a "new order" in China.

renewed. Little appeared in the J: panese press in regard
to the matter but reports from other sources indicated
thet by the end of the month Soviet-Outer Mongolian forces
had succeeded in making large advances across the Halha
River and were strongly attacking defending Japanese troops
some miles east of the river.

(r) General International Relations.
Strengthening of the Anti-Comintern Poct.
It will be remembered that after a long series of
Cabinet conferences this past spring, it was anncunced
that on May 26 a decision hed been arrived at concerning
Japan's policy vis-à-vis the European situation. No
official informetion was given out as to what the policy
was to be but it was understood that it had been then
determined that Japan should not embroil itself directly
with European effairs. Shortly after the first of August
it became known that the wholo matter had been egain
reopened. On August 8 a five-Minister conference was con-
vened to deal with a demand for the strengthening of the
anti-Comintern pact into an alliance of Japan with Germany
and Italy. Certain of the more nationalistic elements in
Japan and the Army had put forward and were strongly pres-
sing a proposal to this end. Publicly, demonstrations of
various org nizations and posters displayed in the streets
of the cities backed up this demand. The press found as
the factors responsible for the reappearance of the sub-
ject the intractability of the British manifested in the
Tokyo conference, the challenging attitude of Soviet Russia,
the danger to Japan that was latent in any successful con-
clusion of the Anglo-Russian talks, and the apprehension
over the United States' exercising pressure against Japan
after the abrogation of the trade treaty.
It was renorted that very serious deliberations in
the Cabinet as well as amongst the other high Government
officials

=== Page 333 === (confidence 0.98)
- 19 -
officials occurred in almost daily conferences over the
Army's proposel up till the day news was recei od of the
con:lrsion of the German-Soviet treaty. Reportedly e
faction of the Government had strongly opposed ury actic
going so far as the adoption of an alliance with the Axis
Powers; but whether or not decision had been arrived at
concerning any policy was not made known. The signing by
Germany of the pact with Russia brought this matter to an
abrupt end. On August 25, following a full Cabinet meeting,
the Chief Secretary of the Cabinet issued a statement that
"in view of the new situation created by the signing of
the German-Soviet non-aggression treaty, the Government
has decided to drop the question of the former Europearn
policy thus wiping Japan's diplomatic slate clean, and
that the Government in regard to future measures would
deliberate from a new point of view.
III. INTERNAL AFFAIRS
(a) Cabinet
1. Rosignation of Hiranumn Cabinet
On the morning of August 28, Premier Kiichiro Hiranuma,
together with his entire Cabinet, resigned. In a statement
issued immediately after his rosignation Baron Hirnnuma,
explaining the reasons for his cction, said that "due to
the signing of the non-aggression pact between Germany and
the Sovict Union a complicated, strange new situation had
developed in Europe which compelled the Government to
formulate a new policy, abandoning completely the policy
that had been preparud" and that since this meant an
alteration

=== Page 334 === (confidence 0.98)
- 20 -
in the position of his Cabinet he could no longer retein
his office.
In the foregoing section of this report there has
been set forth the profound affect that the conclusion
of the non-aggression pact between Germany and Russia
had upon the mind and feelings of the Japanese. It was
felt that the treaty amounted to a titiation of the anti-
Comintern pact, on which Japan's special relations with
Germany and Italy had been based. Now, the situation was
completely changed. Therefore, the resignation of the
Hiranuma Cabinet was to manifest the end of Japan's
former position. It was also to assume joint responsi-
bility for the failure of the Japanese Government to fore-
see the German-Soviet treaty. Moreover, as stated by the
Premier, it was to prepare the way for a new unencumbered
position to be taken up by Japan, divested of any involve-
ments with European affairs and enabled to establish a
new "free and independent policy".*
2. Inauguration of the Abe Cabinet
Immediately upon the resignation of the Hiranuma
Cabinet it was reported that General Nobuyuki Abe, retired,
had been selected to form n new Cabinet. On August 30
Gencral Abe's Cabinet was invested. The new Cabinet was
made up ns follows:
Prime

It is to be noted that four of the key posts were
assumed concurrently, with the Premier himself taking as
well the portfolio of the Foreicn Minister.
The selection of General Abe for the new Prime Minister
was, it is understood, offected by a small group of in-
tangibly powerful men around the Throne. General Abe was
aperson completely unknown in civil affairs and without
particular distinction in the Army. However, he was re-
portedly well liked on all sides and could count on the
support of all elements. He was therefore determined upon
to carry out the new "free and independent policy" that had
been decided upon beforehand. The full' and early settle-
ment of the China affair along the lines already dotermined
upon was to form the keynote of the policy, domestic as
well as foreign. At the same time domestic affairs were
to be placed on a more efficient wartime basis and efforts
were to be made to adjust the country's foreign relations
to conform with Japan's objectives in China.
The membership of Genoral Abe's Cabinet was not con-
sidered by the press as very impressive. Comment in

V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE
On August 25 th: Government issued bonds in the
amount of Yen 500,000,000, of which the Deposit Bureau
took Yen 150,000,000, and the Bank of Japan 350,000,000.
A portion of these bonds was offered to the public through
the Post Office. However, it is understood that bond
sales slackened during the month under review due primarily
to a heavier flow of funds into other Government channels.
It will be recalled* that the Government has been
contemplating drastic revision of the taxation system of
Japan and an increase in the tax revenues. It was announced
by the Home Office on August 24 that instoad of the Yen
500,000,000 increase as previously reported, it was designed
to increase the tex revenues by Yen 1,500,000,000. The
extra billion yen was to be made by an increase in local
tax revenues.
It was also announced during the month that minis-
terial estimates to be submitted for the 1940-4l budget
would amount to around Yen 7,000,000,000, which was to
be larger by Yen 2,450,000,000 then the buaget for the
current fiscal year.
VII. GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS**
Business and financial transactions during August
were characterized by extreme caution and apprehension
as
*Monthly Report for July.
Prepared with the assistance of the office of the
Commercial Attaché.

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.99)
-24 -
as a result of the series of surprising and disturbing
international events. Besides these, other factors tending
to intensify business difficulties were: the sharp break
in the New York-London cross rate; the reports of the
Government's decision to invoke further provisions of the
General Mobilization Law; and the formulation of further
control measures to regulate prices, the supply and dis-
tribution of coal, shipping, shipbuilding, lumber, rice,
and machinery.
It was reported that the Government had decided to
invoke Articles 7 and 8 of the General Mobilization Law
to permit Government inspection of annual accounts of all
concerns capitalized at over l0 million yen with a view to
regulating expenditures and control over distribution of
profits of some 500 major industrial and business organi-
zations.
On August 8 two Imperial Ordinances were promulgated
putting in force provisions of the Rice Distribution
Control Law. These referred to the regulation on cornering
and hoarding of rice, price control, and the distribution
of the sale of rice throughout the country. According to
these regulations, on August 25 the maximum sales price of
rice was set at Yen 38.00 per koku (1 koku being 4.96
bushels).
It was reported at the end of the month that the
Central Price Policy Commission had decided upon the
establishment of a new semi-official coal company, to be
capitalized at Yen 100,000,000, which was aimed to create
a Government monopoly of the distribution of coal. It is
undorstood

=== Page 339 === (confidence 0.99)
- 25 -
understood that this company would have the power to
regulate the product on, distribution, and price of coal
and would be enabled to purchase all equipment and resour
of the existing coal traciig companies. The occasion for
such a concern was, it was reported, the need for the
drastic curtailment in the consumption of coal by the
people as a whole due to the shortage in the fulfillment
of the requirements of the national policy industries, as
well as to make up for these industries the lack of electric
power. In this latter connection, this year's shortage of
water in hydroelectric plants' reservoirs is expected to
greatly diminish the available supply of electric power.
It was reported that gas for heating purposes in the larger
cities would also be curtailed due to a reduction in the
amount of coal to be available for the manufacture of the
gas.
The stock market during the month witnessed wide
fluctuations. Up till the last week the movement was
downward but thereafter some evidence of bullish tendencies
developed from anticipation of a European war. Commodity
prices continued upward during the month. After August 25
silk prices which had been depressed again showed an up-
ward tendency. In a further effort to stem the upward
trend of commodity prices, the Central Price Policy Com-
mission reportedly recommended to the Government the
invocation of all provisions of the General National
Mobilization Law for forceful control of prices. In this
latter connection, it is understood that the new Cabinet
would very likely, in its efforts to reorganize the economy
of

=== Page 340 === (confidence 0.98)
- 26 -
of the nation on a firmer wartime basis, take measures
for the stringent control of these factors.
Money rates renaines stable during the month and no
lack of funds was eviden. Foreign exchange remained at
the rate of US$27-1/2 up to August 25th when, with he
break in the London-New York cross rate, it began to diop
and by the end of the month was approximately US25.
Shipping
It was announced on August 6 that a new shipping
company to be known as the Toa Kaiun Kaisha (ast Asia
Shipping Company) would be established and was to begin
operations in September. This concern was to involve
15 shipping compenies and have 59 ships with a total gross
tonnage of 200,000 under its management. It was designed
to coordinate and consolidate Japanese shipping in the
trade communication system between Japan and China. All
but the tramp and irreguler servicos would come under the
control of the coneern.
It was reported that the Government was seriously
considering taking measures to control production costs
of ships in Japen. it was said that this matter was
becoming a pressing one due to the very heavy increases
in the cost of shipbuilding in Japan. As indicative of
the present cost of shipbuildine, the cost per ton wes as
high as Yen 752.00 for a 1,000 ton freighter, this represen-
ting a 100 percent incrense over 1938, end a 500 percent
increase over the figures before hostilities in China begen.

=== Page 341 === (confidence 0.98)
- 27 -
Foreign Trade
Foreign trade returns for the month of August were
given as exports Yen 377,300,000 and imports Yen 246,196,(
These represented an increase of some Yen 30,000,000 in
exports in value and a decrease of some Yen 20,000,000 in
imports in value in comparison with the month before.
The continued increase in the exports of textiles counted
for a large part in the rise of the exports. Together
with this, however, were also sizeable increases in raw
silks and machinery, and appreciable increases in the
exports of canned provisions. The latter was a seasonable
rise. Exports to the yen bloc and Hongkong were Yen
176,325,000, and imports Yen 4e,518,000, representing a
larger trade in exports and a further drop in imports over
July. The favorable balance of trade, it will be noted,
was practically entirely accounted for by the excess of
exports over imports to the yen bloc; but at the same
time Jepanese exports and imports to and from the rest of
the world were about equal.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Japanese Request for Withdrawel
of Armed Forces
of Belligerent European Nations from Japanese
Occupied Areas in China.
On September 5 the Charge d'Affaires wus called to
the Foreign Office and wes presented by the Vice Minister
for Foreign Affairs with a copy of an identic note that hed
been delivered that day by the Japanese Government to the
representatives of European belligerent powers. Mr. Dooman
was requested to communicate the note to the American Govern.
ment "for its information". The note began with the
statement: "In the face of the war that has just broken
out in Europe, the Japanese Government intends not to
be involved therein, but will concentrate their efforts
on the settlement of the China affair."
There next fol-
lowed a request thet belligerent Governments refrain from
taking any measures that might prcjudice Japan's position
in regurd to the China affair. It was then stated that
the Japanese Government considered it necessary to offer
"a friondly advice" to those Powers "that they should
voluntarily withdraw" their troops and warships from the
regions of China under the control of Japanese forces for
the reason that their presence might "give rise to untoward
incidents and to & situation not in keeping with Japan's
policy of non-involvement". It was added that the Japanese
authorities thereafter would undertake to protect the lives
and property of the nationals of the belligerent powers.
In reply to Mr. Dooman's inquiry, Mr. Sawada stated that
this request unqualifiedly applied to the forces maintained
by the belligerents in the internationel settlements and
foreign concessions.*
No
* Embassy's telegram No. 458, September 5, 7 p.m.

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
No publicity was given in Japan to this "friendly ad-
vice" given to the Briti h and French. Apparently it was
in the nature of a trial balloon or to set the stage for
future action that would iεter publicly be disclosed. In
view of the circumstances, among others the total absence
in China of forces hostile to Britain and France and the
fact that the armed forces of the foreign powers are not
maintained in China for military purposes but to give as-
sistance in the protection of foreign lives there, the
move appeared to be in effect & drastic demand on the Bri-
tish and French Governments to get their forces out of
China, for motives ulterior to those set forth in the note
and directed at undermining the position in China of
western powers.*
The Department in its telegrephic instructions
**
directed the Embassy that it might bring to the attention
of the Foreign Office the fact that any action of the
Japanese Government to force the withdrawal of British
and Franch forces in China would be construed by the
United States as a step in the direction of eliminating
western influence from China. It is noted that the
Secretary on September 6 called in the Japanese Ambassador
at Washington and in a detailed oral statement set forth
in this sense the United States Government's concern in
the matter, pointing out at the same time that the advice
given by the Japanese Government directly affected the
rights and interests of the United States.

the foroeful search on September 1 of the American Consul

had been conducted by Chinese police with the support of
two Japcrese sailors. Mr. Docman in his formal note ad-
dressed to the Foreign Minister in conformity with these
instructions pointed out that the United States Government
could not admit the right of Japanese authorities in China
to seize and search an American consular officer under any
ciroumstances; that the American Government felt that dis-

authorities against the officer who was responsible for
the action in that he did not issue orders that the American
Consul and other American citizens be exempted from search;
and that the American Government looked to the Japanese
Government to take definite and effective measures to stop
entirely such incidents which, it was felt, would not con-
tinue to occur if the Japanese Government issued appropriate
orders and made them effective.*
Shortly after the incident above referred to occurred,
the Americun Vice Consul at Tsingtao, who was at Chefoo on
leave, was also reported to have been searched for the same
purpose by Chinese police agents. Mr. Dooman also took
up this case with the Foreign Office and stressed the fact
that if such occurences should be allowed to continue, they
would not fail to raise an issue of serious character.

at that port since the beginning of hostilities in China.**

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.98)

Japanese-American relations. In fact, by the end of
September these relations had apparently come to take the
leading place in the pub ic eye in regard to Japanese foreiz
affairs. Such attention devoived primarily upon ccntinued
speculation of what the status of American-Japanese relation
would be following the termination of the commercia! treaty
in January 1940; upon reports that feeling against and
opposition to Japan in the United States was continually
growing; upon reports that the United States was increasing
its naval forces and defense preparations in the Pacific;
and upon concern over Japan's immediate and future trade
relations with the United States as they have been and
will be affected by the disruption of Japanese trade with
European countries and their possessions due to the war
and by the greater dependence therefore of Japan upon trade
with the United States.
On September 20 the spokesman of the Foreign Office
gave an interview to the foreign newspaper correspondents
in which he is reported to have stated that although Japan's
policy for setting up a new order in East Asia was "firm and
immutable", the fears that Japan intended to drive American
and other third-power interests out of China were ill-foundod
and ridiculous. He added, however, that in view of extensive
military operations now being carried on throughout China
there might be some instances where the interests of third
powers could not be respected in the manner desired by the
Japanese Government but that with the restoration of peace
in China the rights and interests of other powers there
would be fully respected. He concluded, after referring to
the reports that the United States was increasing its forces
in the Pacific, with the statement that the maintenance or
peace and order in the Far East is Japan's prime wish and if
suct.

=== Page 361 === (confidence 0.97)
- 7-
suoh were threatened by third powers they would have to
answer to Japan for it.
In the letter addressed by Mr. Yoshizawa tc Mr. Doom!
concerning the shipment of American-owned wood oil. frcm
Hankow wnich has been referrel to in a foregoing paragreph,
Mr. Yoshizawa also undertook a long disoussion of the
Japanese position in regard to foreign rights ond intarests
in China vis-&-vis the Japanese. He stated in this regard
that the right of navigation of the Yangtze River is one
which must be considered in connection with the rectification
of such privileged reletionships as the concessions, extra-
territorial rights, et cetera, which exist in China, and
besides the present closing of the Yangtze River wes teing
carried out for strategic reasons in connection with mili-
tary necessities. He also said thet in effect the attitude
of foreign nationals and their governments towards the
Japanese and Japanese policies had much to do with the con-
sideration that was to be given those nationals and their
interests in China.* In a later statement made by Mr.
Yoshizawa he declared that this letter was intended "for
the record only", and addressed itself primarily to the
charge that the Japanese are discriminating against Americon
interests.
At about the same time that the interview of the
Foreign Office spokesman was given there appeared a set of
obviously officially inspired editorials in the press which
predicted the worsening of Japanese-American relations. The
cditorials all were written cround reports of American de-
fense preparations in the Pacific and played on the theme
of charging that the United States was preparing to ussume
re role of watchdog for British and French interests in the
Far East. It was argued that the United States was convind

: Embassy'3 telegram Nos. :470, September 12, 5 p.m., 473,

Japan, in carrying out its program of setting up a new
order in East Asia, would seize the opportunity through
the weakness of the Anglo-French position in the Far Easr
to bring greater pressure to bear on British end Freneh in-
terests here, and that in orer to forestall these develop-
ments the United States was strengthening its naval forces
in the Pacific and Oriental waters to take & hand in the
situation. The editorials were then concluded on the note
that such policy was fraught with the danger of bringing
American and Japanese forces in the Pacific in conflict
with each other, but the hope was added that this could be
avoided through American desire not to have the clouds of
war spread over the Pacific. These editorials, it was learned:
were instigated by the Government primarily to prepare the

With the fall of Kaoan there was initiated a Japanese
campaign on the largest scale that has been witnessed since
the Wuhan operations in l938, a campaign directed agcint
forces of the Chinese Nintn Wer District in the Nanchang-
Yochow-Changsha area. The Japanese reportedly brought in
two new divisions and set out with some seven to nine divi-
sions to attack what the Japanese officially estimated to
be around 400,000 Chinese troops, apparently with the ob-
jective of capturing Changsha. The Japanese started their
offensive in three main attacks: one launched from the
eouthern end of Lake Tungting, another from the Yochow-
Tungcheng line, and the third near the Tayun mountains.
Up to the end of the month the operations had reportedly
resulted in the capture of Tungcheng in southwest Hupeh;
Hsinchiang, Pingkiang and Putang in northern Hunan; and
Siushui and Santu on the Siu River, west of Wuning, in
Kiangsi. By the last day of September, moreover, the
Jap&nese unit which had driven south from Lake Tungting
wa; claimed to have reached within sixteen kilometres of
Changsha. During the campaign a number of heavy engage-
ments were said to have occurred. The Japanese claimed by
the month's end that Chinese losses were over 25,O00 killed
and over 2,000 prisoners, while Jaranese losses amounted to
some 500 killed and 2,000 wounded.
During these operations considerable fighting also
occurred south of Nanchang. Further fighting was also re-
ported in Shansi, Kiangsu, Shantung, Anhwei, Chekiang and
Kwangtung Provinces. The fighting in Shansi appeared to
be a part of the Japanese attempt that had been dragging
on unsuccessfully from month to month to gain military con-
trol of that province. In Kwangtung engagements occurred

(f) General International Relations
1.
Japan's Position in regard to the European
War.
On September 4 the Japanese Government set forth its
stand in regard to the European war in an announcement
released by the Cabinet which merely stated that "the Imperia
Government will not become involved in the conflict ... but
will devote all its energies toward resolving the China in-
cident".
. In reply to inquiries made of the Foreign Office
whether a formal proclamation of neutrality would be made,
it was stated that it was considered that the above-quoted
announcement set forth clearly the position of the Japanese
Government and that no further action was contemplated for
the time being. Universal approval was given to this declara-
tion of non-involvement in
the European hostilities, it
being held that Japan should have nothing to do with either
side in the conflict but devote its attentions to Far Eastern
affairs.
It wes intimated that some significance could be
attached to the fact that the Cabinetayannouncement
was worded
as it was rather than being expressed as a precise declaration
of neutrality. It was suggested in this regard that the
Governnent thereby had more or less
reserved its position,
to give it latitude of action
that it might wish to call
upon in the future in pursuance
its aims in East Asia
or in its position and dealings with the belligerents which
interfered with those aims.
The possibility of the outbreak of hostilities in
Europe was anticipated in Japan as in practically all other
countries.
Therefore when war did break out, although it
was a severe shock, it hardly came
 as a surprise to the
country.
The articulate Jananese feelines concerning the
war were principally confined to Jupan's position.
There
appeared

=== Page 372 === (confidence 0.97)
- 13 -
appeared to be practically no opinions expressed of
partiality or taking sides with one party or the other.
Although much of the blame for the war was laid at the
feet of the Versailles ireety. the whole question of the
conflict was locked uron as a highly confused occurrence
and the complications and outcome entirely unpredictable
and fraught with unforseeable consequences.

(b) Government Proclemations
There was apnroved by the Cabinet on Sepiember 13 Por
early promulgation certain ordinances enforcing provisions
of Articles 8, 1l and 19 of the National Mobilization Law.
The ordinances prohibited for the period of one year the
increase of prices above those prevailing on September 
l939, for general commodities, insurance, storage, lan
and hcuse rentals, processing, wages and salaries. However,
as one exception, house and land rates were to be pegged 
those prevailing on August 4, 1978, rether than at those
of Septe.aber 18, 1939. Only by Government consent could
any changes upward in the prices be made thereafter.
Th sc
provisions, however, were not to apply to the prices of
goods exported to countries except those of the "yen blo..
It was considered that the enforcement of these pre.
visions of the National Mobilization Law constituted the
most drastic ster undertaken thus far in the control of th.
economic life of Japan.
The move was not altogether un-
expected as it had been under study from almost the berir.
ning of the year.
However, the dislocation of the pric
level in Japan brought about by the outbreak of hostilitiee
in Europe which resulted in sharp inereases in commodity
prices partially through speculation waa a contributory
factor in the decision to enforce the provisions at this
time.
Much conjecture was voiced in Japan as to whether
the stabilization of prices at a set level would be able in
fact to withstand the pressure that undoubtedly would to
brought to bear on them by financial and economic conditin
abroad; but still it was felt that, nevortheless, the
measure was a necessary one.

VI. GENERAL EC(NOMIC AND FIHANCIAL CONDITIONS *
The outbreak of European hostilities, following upon
94.50
the series of disturbing incidents to Japan that occurred
in July and August greatly augmented the already numerous
and perplexing problems facing the Government, business and
finance. An immediate reaction to the war was the staging
of a very enthusiastic demonstration in the Tokyo Stock
Exchange, where prices soared on Sertember 4 by from Yen 1.00
to Yen 26.00 with shipping shares in the lead followed by
fibrous groups, other export industries and shipbuilding
issues. Commodity prices also firmed immediately.
The abnormal rises in shares and goods caused grave
concern in official querters to the extent that stens wor
at once taken to counteract a dangerous price inflation.

The surge of initial enthusiasm at the beginning of
the month soon fell off as it began to be realized that
Japan was not in as good a position as it was first sup-
posed to take advantage of the opportunities offered by
the war for a business boom. In taking stock of its
position there were found adverse fators as well as
favorable ones to be considered.
The factors favorable for an increase in Jaran's foreign
trade as
a result of the European hostilities were considered
to be (l) the opportunity for Japan to increase its sales
abroad of agricultural, marine and manufactured products
by taking up the markets formerly held by the belligerent
Powers and by even increasing sales of
those products to
the belligerent Powers themselves; (2)
the opportunity
to realize much higher prices for Japanese exnorts through
the general
increase in world prices; and (3) the oppor-
tunity to make large profits from its shipping industry
through the withdrawal of competition of much of the shipping
of the belligerent Powers and through the inreased demand
in world commerce for Japanese ships.

some producing countries on those raw materia s ana the

materials as oil, copper, aluminum, nickel, iron ores,

Japan's chances of obtaining the necessary materials where-

the markets with their demands. Secondly, it was realized
that a number of foreign countries directly or indirectly

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
January 19, 1940
Tokyo's despatch no. 4269, November 30,
1939, entitled "Report of Conditions in
Japan During the Month of October 1939".
In a press interview on October 30 the
Japanese who 1s Vice Governor of the Hua Hsin
Commeroial Bank was alleged to have stated
that the new regime when established will not
recognize the Chinese national currency,
whioh will be "wiped out of existence".
In mid-October there was held at Tokyo
an economic conference of representatives of
Japan, "Manchukuo", and north China for the
purpose of examining the entire industrial
structure of the yen bloo. Some hundred
delegates attended. In connection with the
opening of the conference there appeared in
the press various articles interpreting the
"new order in East Asia" as a Far Eastern
economic bloc necessary to Japan because of
the disappearance of free trade in inter-
national relations. It was stated that the
Far Eastern economic bloc could be made self-
sufficient in almost all major resources
needed for present-day economic life except
(1) petroleum, (2) copper, and (3) rubber.
On October 3 the Japanese War Office
officially announced that Japanese casualties
suffered during the Nomonhan fighting had
amounted to 18,000. This release came as a
startling

=== Page 389 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
startling bolt to the Japanese public and has
apparently undermined the assumption of the
Japanese public that the Japanese Army 1s
easily superior to the Soviet Army.
Replacement of Mr. Shiratori as Japanese
Ambassador to Italy and of General Oshima as
Ambassador to Germany (the new ambassador to
Germany being Mr. Kurusu) marks a return on
the part of Japan to a more formalized state
of diplomatic relations with Germany and
Italy.
At page 26 is a brief statistical state-
ment of the Japanese national debt and of
flotation of bonds. It was reported that
when an 1ssue of 600,000,000 yen in national
bonds was offered in October, only 47.9 per-
cent of the bonds issued during the month
were assimilated.
On October 24 the Japanese Government
officially confirmed that the yen was pegged
to the United States dollar. It had formerly
been pegged to the pound. The rate 1s
23-7/16 cents.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES
(a)
Ambassador's Address Before the America-
Japan Society.
Upon his return to Tokyo, on October 10, from leave of
absence in the United States, the Ambassador delivered before
123861
the America-Japan Society at a luncheon held on October i9 a
most significant and outstanding aadress on American - Japanese
relations. The speech was & plain and direct exposition of
the American attitude and stand tcwards Japanese actions and
policies concerning China and brcught into clear relief the
issues between the two courtries that had arisen in that
regard. It represented a definite nark in the United States'
diplomatic relations with Japan, ranking in significance with
the Amer ican Govern ent's notes of October 6 and December 30,
1938; but this step carried far-reaching significance in
that it presented to the Japanese poople for the first tine
in precise forn the entire scope of the probleus of American-
Japanese relations.
The addreis in its entirety was made
public and printed in full in all the Japanese press.
A brief resume or the text" may be iven as follows:
After stressing the weicht of American public opinion on the
policy and actions of its Gevernment and the fact that Ameri-
can public opinicn was hased on facts and a studied examina-
tion of the best of infornation, ir. Grew stated that the
American people had come to the unanimous opinion of strongly
resenting some or the thin's the Japanese armed forces are
now doing in China, iucluding thoce arainst Anrican rights
and interests. In then settin forth the position of the
Am rican people, he pointed cut that the pulicy of the United
States is based on a fervent desire for internat'onal peace
resting
Erbassy's despatch no. 4186, Cctober 1, 1939.

=== Page 395 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
resting upon orderly processes in international dealings.
He said that another cardinal principle of American policy
is equality of commercial opportunity. He noted that this
was not a policy maintained by the United States alone
inasmuch as Japan itselr hed also insisted upon the open
door in various parts of the world other than China. He
then spoke of the carge often heard in Japan that the
American Government and people did not "understand the
New Order in East Asi&" and declared that this was not the
case. He noted that the new order had been officially
defined by Japan as an order of security, stability and
progress, and that as such it was fully understood and
appreciated by the United States. He pointed out, however,
that with this program there had been included other things
which the American people could not accept. He then set
forth in general terms instances of the violation of American
rights and int rests, the bombings and other damaging of
American property, the impairment of American commercial and
economic interests and the infliction of personal indignities
on American citizens. He said, moreover, that the American
people had good reason to believe from reliable evidence that
Japan was endeavoring to
establish control in China for
Japan's own and sole interst by a system of closed and
exclusive economy. In conclud ing his address he made a
vigorous appeal for a removal of the causes of the issues
between the United States and Japan in order that an essen-
tial improvement might be attained in the friendly relations
between the two countries.
The

=== Page 396 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
The volume of press comment concerning the address
that appeared immediately after its delivery, and the arrey
of editorials and feature articles in the newspapers giving
searching studies of the contonts of the speech which fol-
lowed the release of the full text and which continued
throughout the rest of the month, were indicative of the
profound effect it had in Japan.
The first reaction was one of deep shock and bewilder-
ment and the immediate recoil from this initial effect took
the form of a wounded and defensive attitude. Ianoring en-
tirely the primary question raised by Mr. Grew of the damage
to American rights anc interests by the Jananese military
in China, and disregarding the fact that the American Govern-
ment had already set forth its stand that treatics were not
forever unalterable, the press declared that Mr. Grew's
address as a formal expression of American official opinion
showed that the United States had failed to realize that
the Nine Power Treaty ccnel uded twenty years ago was no
longer applicable to Eastern Asia, was anachronistic and
completely out of keering with the changed situation that
had come ahout in this rart of the world, that the United
States which has itself set up srecial political and
economic arrangements in jouth America, still failed to
take into consideration what Japun was attempting to do
with regard to the "new order', and that, al though in the
United States' opposition, Janan would "find thorns in its
path" in attainins its aims, nevertheless there would not
be the slightest deviation from its "immutable policy".
Some of the more extremist views put forward characterized
the address as tynical of American arrogance and insolence
and

=== Page 397 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
and averred that following this open announcement of America's
oprosition to Janan this country should give up "its boot-
licking diplomacy toward the United States" wherein Japan had
maintained a servile attitude of making every effort to court
American favor and to defer to American wishes.
After a lapse of a few days and after the full text of
the speech had become known, a more studied and reasoned view
appeared in the comments upon it. Acknowledgment was made of
the frank, accurate picture of the American attitude toward
Japan which Mr. Grew had painted and it was said that the
speech was courageous and to be apnreciated. There were heard
on many sides requests for enlightenment as to the exact
character of the damage sustained by Americans in China and
encroachment on their interests. There was also in evidence
some recognition that there may have been a good deal of
justification in Amorican complaints regarding the treatment
of its rights and interests in China, and a demand developed
in some quarters that concrete steps be taken to repair the
unfavorable relations with the United 3tates. Other oninions
voiced were to the effect that Japan should change its views
with regard to the American position. In this last connectior
a significant assertion was made that Janan had been resting
under a delusion that the abrogation of the Treaty of Commerce
and Navigation was an action taken by the United States solely
for domestic and political purposes.*

(b) Extra Settlement Roads Area, Shanghai.
In conformity with che Department's instructions, *
the Emra:y made representations to the Foreign Office in
regard to the situation that hnd arisen with respect to the
893.102
attempts by the Japanese-sponsored municipal regime at
Shanghai to encrcach upon and interfere with the functions
of the police of the International Settlement on the extra-
Settlement roads in the western part of Shanghai. It was
pointed out in the Embassy's note ** to the Foreign Office
that some 500 American citizens are residing in the western
part of Shanghai and that therefore the American Government
is seriously concerned over the maintenance of peace and
order in that area. In this latter connection it was
observed that the enclaves between the roads had become
rendezvous of lawless and vicious elements and was also
infested with disreputable establishments. It was requested
that the Jananese Government use its influence with the
Japanese-sponsored regime to the end that that regime elimi.
nate the lawless elements and by arrangement with the Settle-
ment authorities remove the friction over the policing of
the extra-Settlement roads.
By note dated Cctcber 26, the Foreign Office replied
to these representations stating that the Japanese Consul
General at Shanghai had been instructed to use his good
offices in the matter.***

(c) International Settlement at Kulangsu, Amoy.
It will be recalled that since May of this year negotia-
tions have been held for settlement of the issue that arose
last spring over the International Settlement at Kulangsu,
Amoy. On October 16 the Enbassy was informed that the final
draft of an agreement between the Japanese authorities and
the Kulangsu Municipal Council had been approved by the Japanes
Government. On the following day the agreement was reached at
Kulangsu. On October 18 the Japanese and American Marines -
the British and French Marines having departed during Septem-
ber - left the Settlement. *
Comment on the settlement in
the Japanese press was to the effect that it was to be wel-
comed not only as a means of establishing peace and order in
the Settlement but also to show the way to approach the problen
of the Settlements at Shanghai and Tientsin. It was further
added that the lesson taught was that there were few problems
arising from the China incident which would not yield to
solution if taten from the view of accenting the new situaticn
in China.

VI. GENERAL ECCNOMIC AIT. FINANCIAL CCIIDITIONS *
Although during the month under review business in Jaran
was generally good and active, having received considerable
stimulus from the war in Europe, improvement was still being
retarded by losses of exnort markets, low stocks of raw
materials for export oods, advancin base prices abroad of
essential imrorts, higher transnortation costs, delayed deli-
veries cnd serious domestic shortage of coal and of electric
power. Numereus brances of trade were worried over obtain-
ing supplies of necess ry raw ma erials, such as wool from
Australia, cotton from India, scran iron, stael and machinery
from the United states, and potash and rulp from Europe.
Exporters of sundry ecods were uneasy over the disposition
of their products due to so .any closad murkets. Fusiness
and comercial circles were worr led over vhat action the
United states intended to tc'e following the ter ination of
the Co mercial treaty with Ja on, for the latter is now
dependant upon the former for the bulk of her import require-
nents. Still another factor causing grcwing concern in Janan
was the insufficient status of nlant machinery caused by lack
